Discussion Name:
Should the government provide funding for arts programs?

1. Thesis:
The government should provide funding for arts programs.

  1.1. Pro:
  Involvement in the arts can be a positive influence on vulnerable people in society.

    1.1.1. Con:
    The government should be providing vulnerable citizens [1] with access to education, employment opportunities, healthcare, and mental health services, not arts programs.

      1.1.1.1. Con:
      People with mental challenges can benefit from art therapy [2] as part of a holistic approach to mental health.

        1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
        Arts programs directly contribute to the mental health and welfare [3] of vulnerable veterans.

        1.1.1.1.2. Con:
        Research on the effectiveness of art therapy is overwhelmingly qualitative and, therefore, unsupported in comparison with traditional therapeutic modalities (p. 11 [4]).

      1.1.1.2. Pro:
      By definition, vulnerable groups [5] are in danger of going without basic necessities, and art is not a necessity [6].

        1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
        Programs that provide basic necessities to vulnerable populations have shown significant benefits to health improvement and long-term outcomes (pp. 591, 597 [7]).

        1.1.1.2.2. Con:
        Providing vulnerable groups with access to the arts does not detract from the work of programs devoted to addressing their basic needs; both goals can work in tandem.

    1.1.2. Pro:
    Arts programs funded by the government can contribute to the cognitive and social health of senior citizens.

      1.1.2.1. Pro:
      Arts programs have been shown to improve the emotional health and well-being of older adults, particularly with regard to depression and isolation (p. 42 [8]).

        1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
        In Germany, where arts funding is significantly higher [9] than in the US, only 5% of older persons are diagnosed with depression [10], compared with 20% in the US.

          1.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
          There are numerous other factors that may be responsible for depression among older Americans. For example, the high rate of diabetes [11] among older adults in the US may contribute to higher rates of depression.

        1.1.2.1.2. Pro:
        Older adults are at high risk of social isolation, which has significant implications for their health and longevity (p. 1 [12]).

        1.1.2.1.3. Con:
        The improved health of older adults in arts programs may be linked to the social nature of the activity rather than its artistic content. Nonartistic social activities [13] could provide as much aid in reducing social isolation.

      1.1.2.2. Pro:
      Participatory arts programs can improve cognitive function in older adults (p. 751 [14]).

        1.1.2.2.1. Con:
        Research indicates that activities involving physical exercise [15] are more effective at preventing dementia in older adults.

        1.1.2.2.2. Pro:
        Programs that improve cognitive function in older adults have significant potential to improve the quality of life of the expanding older population [16] (p. 199 [17]).

      1.1.2.3. Con:
      The government should fund social welfare and public healthcare programs for senior citizens instead of arts programs.

        1.1.2.3.1. Con:
        Arts programs for seniors can bring substantial health benefits (p. 363 [18]), which can reduce the financial burden associated with long-term care services (p. 6 [19]).

        1.1.2.3.2. Pro:
        Increased investment in social welfare can extend the life of senior citizens (p. 1 [20]).

      1.1.2.4. Con:
      Society has a tendency to treat older adults as children [21]; some older adults may consider arts programs childish and take offense at the suggestion that such activities are appropriate for them.

        1.1.2.4.1. Pro:
        The infantilization of older adults can constitute abuse and contribute to cognitive issues (pp. 144, 146 [22]).

        1.1.2.4.2. Con:
        The participation of older adults in artistic activities is entirely voluntary and can make significant contributions to their quality of life (p. 938 [23]).

    1.1.3. Pro:
    Arts programs can significantly benefit people caught in the criminal justice system and those at risk of becoming so.

      1.1.3.1. Pro:
      Government-funded arts programs in prisons have been effective in supporting the mental health and rehabilitation of incarcerated people.

        1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
        The government-funded Arts in Correction [24] program supports prisoner rehabilitation, improves the environment of prisons, and helps inmates learn skills that can help them readjust upon release.

        1.1.3.1.2. Con:
        The government should not spend money on arts programs for convicted criminals, as the purpose of prison is to punish offenders (pp. 1, 21 [25]).

        1.1.3.1.3. Pro:
        Prison arts programs can support a restorative view of the criminal justice system [26], allowing convicts a rare opportunity for self-reflection and awareness.

        1.1.3.1.4. Con:
        The families of victims have criticized prison arts programs, claiming that they legitimize criminal behavior [27]; programs that support criminals in creating artworks while incarcerated have been met with outrage in some quarters (pp. 581–582 [28]).

      1.1.3.2. Pro:
      Troubled young people can benefit from government funding for arts programs.

        1.1.3.2.1. Con:
        Arts programs do not directly address the risk factors facing troubled youth (p. 6 [29]); more direct interventions may be more effective.

        1.1.3.2.2. Con:
        Arts programs that focus on at-risk youth can encourage them to pursue art instead of focusing on education and employment [30], which would be more beneficial.

        1.1.3.2.3. Pro:
        Providing outlets for, and examples of, creative expression can have a positive effect [31] on those who might otherwise be likely to express negative emotions through violence or other criminal activity (p. 47 [32]).

          1.1.3.2.3.1. Pro:
          One of the most famous graffiti artists, Jean-Michel Basquiat, began tagging in Brooklyn after a tumultuous childhood [33].

        1.1.3.2.4. Pro:
        The US Department of Justice has supported The Graffiti Arts Project [34] (GAP), a successful intervention for young people in the justice system, which has helped develop artistic expression, prevent delinquency, and promote property reclamation.

      1.1.3.3. Con:
      Prisoners can be positively impacted by participating in educational programs and professional training.

        1.1.3.3.1. Pro:
        Participation in any type of education program during incarceration is associated with a dramatic decrease in recidivism [35].

          1.1.3.3.1.1. Pro:
          Higher levels of educational attainment [36] correlate with lower rates of recidivism.

          1.1.3.3.1.2. Con:
          Since the evidence shows that participation in any education program decreases an inmate's chances of returning to prison, art education programs can offer the same benefit.

          1.1.3.3.1.3. Pro:
          The rate of unemployment is high among prisoners before and after incarceration (p. 10 [37]); prison education programs can lead to additional employment opportunities that can benefit both inmates and society at large.

        1.1.3.3.2. Pro:
        Prisons with college-level education programs experience fewer violent incidents, making them safer for both inmates and corrections officers (p. 1 [38]).

        1.1.3.3.3. Con:
        Many inmates cite their participation in arts programs as a factor in their decisions to participate in other educational opportunities during incarceration (p. 24 [39]).

      1.1.3.4. Con:
      Forgoing the prosecution [40] of nonviolent, misdemeanor crimes results in greater reductions of prison populations and crime than prison arts programs.

        1.1.3.4.1. Con:
        Reducing crime and prison populations does not eradicate the need to support vulnerable groups in the justice system; the approach supported by the parent claim is not mutually exclusive with funding arts programs.

        1.1.3.4.2. Pro:
        It is more productive to focus on preventing people from becoming caught in the criminal justice system in the first place rather than trying to support them after the fact.

    1.1.4. Con:
    Government funding for arts programs that society opposes might turn public opinion against the arts.

      1.1.4.1. Pro:
      Many people hold narrow views of what should constitute art, and may be opposed to programs that seek to expand access to marginalized groups and styles.

        1.1.4.1.1. Pro:
        A great deal of the anti-NEA sentiment commonly found among US conservatives is rooted in historical NEA support of openly homosexual art and artists [41].

          1.1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
          The US government has been criticized on a number of occasions for subsidizing artworks portraying or exploring LGBT+ issues [42], on the grounds that American citizens could find them offensive [43].

          1.1.4.1.1.2. Con:
          Some artists, like Robert Mapplethorpe, create art containing extremely graphic and intense [44] images that would be considered inappropriate even if the subjects were not members of oppressed communities.

        1.1.4.1.2. Con:
        Government-funded arts programs that focus on marginalized groups can help to expand such narrow views and promote inclusivity (p. 69 [45]).

      1.1.4.2. Con:
      This would become less of an issue if government funding of arts programs were increased; the public will be satisfied so long as there are programs that support the styles, artists, or groups that they prefer.

        1.1.4.2.1. Pro:
        People are less likely to object to government-funded art that they disagree with if there is also government-funded art that they believe represents their perspective and interests.

        1.1.4.2.2. Con:
        Regardless of whether there is equal representation of different perspectives, many people will be dissatisfied with the existence of any government-funded artwork that makes a statement to which they are morally opposed [46].

      1.1.4.3. Con:
      The public is likely to hold the government rather than the arts sector responsible for supporting questionable artistic endeavors [47].

        1.1.4.3.1. Pro:
        The government is capable of bearing responsibility for supporting unpopular arts programs that are demonstrably beneficial rather than submitting to the tyranny of the majority [48].

        1.1.4.3.2. Con:
        In some cases, individuals have vandalized or destroyed government-funded art [49] that offended them rather than relying on the government to mediate the situation.

      1.1.4.4. Pro:
      If the government funds arts programs that provoke backlash [50], this may lead to protests or boycotts of museums, galleries, and public art exhibitions.

        1.1.4.4.1. Pro:
        There were widespread calls to boycott art and cultural events and institutions [51] supported by the Turkish government in response to Turkey's 2019 invasion of Syria [52].

        1.1.4.4.2. Con:
        Artists and arts programs themselves are often responsible for instituting boycotts [53] as a means of direct economic action.

    1.1.5. Con:
    Arts funding mostly benefits cultural elitists [54].

      1.1.5.1. Pro:
      Artworks and the art world themselves are inherently elitist, exclusionary, and undemocratic [55].

        1.1.5.1.1. Pro:
        A great deal of art is self-referential and only holds meaning for a limited, elite community [56].

        1.1.5.1.2. Con:
        The art world has become more widely accessible because of the internet and social media [57].

      1.1.5.2. Pro:
      Tax breaks on private support of arts programs allow the wealthy to avoid paying taxes by writing off expensive purchases [58] like gala tickets and charity auction items.

        1.1.5.2.1. Con:
        There is a maximum allowable tax deduction [59] for charitable purchases, so the wealthy cannot completely avoid paying taxes by supporting arts programs.

        1.1.5.2.2. Pro:
        Wealthy art collectors have used tax breaks and exemptions to offset the cost of their private collections by opening subsidized galleries and museums with limited public access [60].

      1.1.5.3. Pro:
      The arts are primarily consumed by the middle and upper-middle class with above-average income (p. 321 [61]).

        1.1.5.3.1. Pro:
        Government funding for the arts is a form of elite welfare [62]; the middle and upper classes can afford to pay for arts programs on their own.

          1.1.5.3.1.1. Pro:
          The states that spend the most per capita on the arts are some of the wealthiest liberal states [63] in America.

          1.1.5.3.1.2. Con:
          The ability to afford something is not the same as willingness to pay for it. Many arts programs would be left unsupported if government subsidies were taken away and the middle and upper classes declined to fill the gaps in funding.

        1.1.5.3.2. Con:
        Lower-income and marginalized communities also benefit [64] from government funding for the arts.

        1.1.5.3.3. Pro:
        Government-funded arts programs increase the participation gap [65] between the wealthy and the poor in the arts and culture [66] industry.

          1.1.5.3.3.1. Con:
          Wealthy people inherently have more disposable income [67] to spend on the arts than poor people.

          1.1.5.3.3.2. Con:
          Participation in the arts follows a different pattern than consumption of the arts and is driven more by education than by socioeconomic class (p. 15 [68]).

          1.1.5.3.3.3. Pro:
          The rise of unpaid internships and low-paying gallery assistantships in the art world relegates these jobs to wealthy people who have the ability to survive with a low or nonpaying entry-level job. [69]

          1.1.5.3.3.4. Pro:
          The buying and selling of artwork is often associated with the desire to publicly display [70] the ability to spend extreme amounts of money on relatively worthless products [71] as a way of maintaining or securing social status.

        1.1.5.3.4. Con:
        If the arts were incentivized in schools, poorer children could grow up with an appreciation for the arts.

      1.1.5.4. Con:
      Many underprivileged people have little opportunity to participate in the arts (p. 1 [72]). Government funding of community-based arts programs can address this issue.

        1.1.5.4.1. Pro:
        In California, over 90% of government-funded arts performances have free admission; funding makes these exhibitions available to people who cannot afford admission (p. 13 [73]).

        1.1.5.4.2. Con:
        The cost [74] of attending an exhibition of government-subsidized art does not differ from that of unsubsidized art, so those from lower socioeconomic backgrounds are not provided with any additional access.

      1.1.5.5. Con:
      The great popularity of graffiti and street art [75] offers an excellent opportunity to fund art that is more accessible to the public.

        1.1.5.5.1. Con:
        Graffiti is already common in many areas and often carries negative criminal associations [76].

          1.1.5.5.1.1. Con:
          Murals and other forms of graffiti are widely accepted as art, and many graffitied streets become tourist attractions [77].

          1.1.5.5.1.2. Pro:
          Graffiti is associated with other property crimes and results in US$12 billion in annual cleanup costs in the US (p. 2 [78]).

        1.1.5.5.2. Pro:
        Urban areas can benefit from government-funded street and graffiti arts programs that beautify the local environment [79].

          1.1.5.5.2.1. Con:
          Graffiti can dramatically lower property values, so it may be unwelcome in many urban areas (p. 2 [80]).

          1.1.5.5.2.2. Pro:
          The Swedish government commissioned graffiti murals [81] in Stockholm metro stations in the 1950s; now, around 90% of the stations have painted walls, murals, and sanctioned graffiti.

      1.1.5.6. Con:
      Privatized arts funding [82] is more likely to beget elitist art than government funding is.

        1.1.5.6.1. Pro:
        In the UK, decreased state education funding risks leaving only privately funded [83] schools and their students with access to arts education.

        1.1.5.6.2. Pro:
        Wealthy donors and corporations are not answerable to society's needs [84] in the same way that governments are.

        1.1.5.6.3. Con:
        Art collectives, such as TrustoCorp [85], have created public art that is broadly accessible [86] and funded by private auctions.

        1.1.5.6.4. Con:
        Government funding [87] is likely to focus more on elite forms of art, with which most people do not engage.

          1.1.5.6.4.1. Pro:
          Government funding often supports elitist art forms [88] that promote gatekeeping [89] by critics and the high-art world.

    1.1.6. Pro:
    The arts are essential for children and adolescents during the formative years of their youth.

      1.1.6.1. Con:
      The arts can expose children to inappropriate influences.

        1.1.6.1.1. Pro:
        Students sometimes produce artwork that is considered controversial [90] for their age or in a particular setting.

        1.1.6.1.2. Pro:
        Museums and galleries can contain exhibits that some parents may consider age-inappropriate or offensive [91].

          1.1.6.1.2.1. Pro:
          Many museums feature nudity or violence [92] in paintings and sculptures, which many parents would find objectionable.

          1.1.6.1.2.2. Con:
          Children can just as easily encounter objectionable material on the internet, where parents are less able to control what their children see.

        1.1.6.1.3. Con:
        The arts offer children an essential avenue for engaging with and exploring controversial topics [93] and learning to express themselves [94].

      1.1.6.2. Con:
      Arts programs in schools detract from children's education.

        1.1.6.2.1. Pro:
        Students often have insufficient time for studying [95]; taking on additional extracurricular activities, such as arts programs, only exacerbates this problem.

        1.1.6.2.2. Con:
        Arts programs in schools can support rather than detract from education; by offering a way for students to express themselves and their energy, arts programs can function as a therapeutic activity [96] to help students who are stressed [97] out by their academic workloads [98].

          1.1.6.2.2.1. Con:
          Therapy from licensed psychologists [99] or reduced academic loads would be more beneficial to students experiencing workload-related stress.

          1.1.6.2.2.2. Pro:
          School-based arts programs can help students cope with difficult situations [100] in their home lives that may be affecting their performance at school.

        1.1.6.2.3. Con:
        Arts programs can be scheduled around regular coursework or outside of normal school hours so that they do not interfere with other subjects.

        1.1.6.2.4. Pro:
        Funding that supports arts programs in schools would be better spent on education in engineering and computer science [101], which are more likely to benefit students in the long run.

      1.1.6.3. Pro:
      Students need a well-rounded education [102] that includes the arts. For many, this would be impossible without government-funded arts programs.

        1.1.6.3.1. Con:
        Arts programs are not a necessary part of academia.

          1.1.6.3.1.1. Pro:
          Only a small percentage of secondary school graduates have been adequately prepared for higher education or employment [103]; this is a more pressing concern than arts education.

          1.1.6.3.1.2. Con:
          Even if a subject is not included in school curricula, this does not mean it is unnecessary. Many essential skills and subjects [104] are not taught in schools.

          1.1.6.3.1.3. Con:
          Consistent student participation in well-designed, long-term arts programs [105] increases their likelihood of high achievement throughout their academic careers.

        1.1.6.3.2. Con:
        If the arts are truly an essential part of one's education, then art programs should be integrated with other subjects [106] rather than provided via separate government-funded programs.

        1.1.6.3.3. Pro:
        Without government funding, chronically underfunded [107] public schools will be unable to offer as comprehensive an arts education as private schools, which deprives students of equal opportunities (p. 38 [108]).

          1.1.6.3.3.1. Con:
          Since private school parents are paying more [109] for their children's education, they should expect that their children will be afforded additional educational opportunities.

          1.1.6.3.3.2. Pro:
          Since private schools generally allocate more time to noncore subjects like art (p. 23 [110]), without additional government funding, only the children of wealthy families will be exposed to art.

        1.1.6.3.4. Pro:
        Arts programs are an essential element of holistic education [111] for young students.

          1.1.6.3.4.1. Pro:
          Integrating government-funded arts programs into educational settings improves the cognitive and socioemotional capabilities [112] of the future workforce.

      1.1.6.4. Pro:
      Government funding for school arts programs bolsters overall academic progress.

        1.1.6.4.1. Pro:
        Art has a positive effect on memory that transfers to other subjects (p. 19 [113]).

          1.1.6.4.1.1. Con:
          There are other ways to improve memory [114] that are simpler and cost less than arts programs.

        1.1.6.4.2. Con:
        There is no reason why arts programs need to be instrumentally justified based on their relevance to other subjects; no other academic subject is held to this standard (p. 3 [115]).

        1.1.6.4.3. Pro:
        Involvement in the arts boosts cognitive skills [116] in children, contributing to their academic achievement.

          1.1.6.4.3.1. Con:
          Sport, outdoor recreation, and field trips are equally, if not more, important than the arts in terms of boosting cognitive skills [117] in children, so funding devoted to arts programs may be better spent in these categories.

          1.1.6.4.3.2. Pro:
          The arts reinforce skills [118] like creativity, problem-solving, and receptivity to constructive feedback.

          1.1.6.4.3.3. Pro:
          The arts teach cognitive skills [119] that are less commonly found in other subjects. Such skills contribute to students' understanding of other disciplines and enhance their ability to meaningfully organize information (p. 24 [120]).

        1.1.6.4.4. Con:
        Government funds supporting arts programs in schools would be better spent on additional teaching staff; this would have a greater impact on student performance (p. 11 [121]).

          1.1.6.4.4.1. Pro:
          When funding is limited, and academic performance is of the utmost importance, then money should be directed at the most effective methods of improving academic performance.

          1.1.6.4.4.2. Con:
          Academic performance is only one element of student outcomes; participation in school arts programs is associated with increased writing achievement, compassion for others, and school engagement (p. 14 [122]).

  1.2. Pro:
  Government spending on arts programs and organizations generates economic activity.

    1.2.1. Pro:
    Government funding of the arts creates employment opportunities.

      1.2.1.1. Pro:
      FDR's Federal Art Project (FAP) employed over 10,000 artists [123], providing vital widespread economic stimulation at a crucial time.

        1.2.1.1.1. Con:
        Some argue that the FAP was a form of US propaganda [124] to spread American ideals during a period of worldwide war and instability—describing the program as being unconcerned with artist employment levels.

        1.2.1.1.2. Con:
        The government placed limitations [125] on the art that could be produced by its army of hired artists: no nudes or direct political statements were allowed.

        1.2.1.1.3. Pro:
        FAP-funded artists produced some of the most iconic works [126] depicting the American experience of that era.

        1.2.1.1.4. Pro:
        The FAP operated more than 100 community art centers and employed artists as teachers [127] in art education outreach programs.

      1.2.1.2. Pro:
      The arts and culture industries in the US employ nearly 5 million people (p. viii [128]). Public funding makes these jobs less susceptible to economic fluctuations, such as recession [129], than would private funding.

        1.2.1.2.1. Pro:
        When the arts are privately funded, jobs are more affected by economic fluctuations, such as recessions [130].

        1.2.1.2.2. Con:
        Ongoing public funding of the arts and culture industry discourages competition and creates complacency [131].

      1.2.1.3. Con:
      Government funding for job creation is better directed at stimulating aggregate demand [132] at the national level to sustain high levels of employment.

        1.2.1.3.1. Con:
        Government-funded arts programs are not the sole method of addressing employment levels; other programs can focus on stimulating aggregate demand at the national level.

        1.2.1.3.2. Pro:
        Tax breaks from the federal government create hiring incentives [133] for employers, which is a functional, proven method of increasing employment opportunities.

      1.2.1.4. Con:
      Virtually any government spending will lead to employment opportunities; there is no reason to single out the arts as particularly useful for job creation.

        1.2.1.4.1. Pro:
        Encouraging economic diversity [134] by creating job opportunities across a spectrum of industries would yield lower unemployment and greater stability.

        1.2.1.4.2. Con:
        The arts offer an excellent opportunity for creating jobs, since one additional job in the arts results in the creation of 1.62 jobs in other sectors (p. 1 [135]).

    1.2.2. Con:
    The arts may stimulate activity but not growth, which slows when it crowds out more productive investments.

      1.2.2.1. Pro:
      When one accounts for inflation, the size of the art market decreased from 2008 to 2018 [136].

        1.2.2.1.1. Pro:
        Many other industries showed significant growth during the same period [137], even when accounting for inflation.

        1.2.2.1.2. Con:
        The online art sales market continues to grow dramatically, with the value of the sector doubling from 2019 to 2021 (p. 36 [138]).

      1.2.2.2. Con:
      Government funding of arts programs is an investment in culture, which bolsters the economy and creates economic growth [139].

        1.2.2.2.1. Con:
        A great deal of art is developed independent [140] of government funding, so the impact of such art must also be considered when evaluating the economic contributions of the arts.

        1.2.2.2.2. Pro:
        The US government funds arts programs to the tune of US$292 million [141] per year, and economic growth [142] in the arts sector has continued to flourish.

      1.2.2.3. Con:
      Cities with a higher Creative Vitality Index [143] experienced more growth in GDP between 2010 and 2015.

        1.2.2.3.1. Con:
        Not every area with strong economic growth has a thriving arts community; geographic location and education level [144] are more strongly associated with GDP growth.

        1.2.2.3.2. Pro:
        Cities with a more robust arts sector are able to generate revenue from a broader range of artistic forms [145].

      1.2.2.4. Pro:
      If the government is seeking to maximize economic growth, investing in other industries like consulting firms or software businesses [146] would be more productive.

        1.2.2.4.1. Con:
        In one study [147], 97% of employees cited that creativity has increasing importance in workplace innovation, which bolsters productivity and company growth.

          1.2.2.4.1.1. Pro:
          Art often plays an important role in modern branding and marketing campaigns [148].

        1.2.2.4.2. Pro:
        Five of the top twenty fastest-growing industries in the US are related to healthcare and social assistance [149]; investing in these industries is sure to be productive.

    1.2.3. Pro:
    Greater spending on arts and culture is linked to better tourist economies [150].

      1.2.3.1. Con:
      Tourists generally spend more on accommodations, food, and shopping than they do on arts and culture (p. 7 [151]); efforts to improve tourist economies should focus on these categories.

        1.2.3.1.1. Con:
        Arts and culture drives spending in other categories, since tourists who travel to experience arts and culture also eat, sleep, and shop.

        1.2.3.1.2. Pro:
        The postpandemic global tourism industry [152] is expected to rebound strongly and create over 126 million new jobs in travel and hospitality.

      1.2.3.2. Pro:
      Tourists visiting city entertainment and arts districts are willing to spend more money than residents, generating additional revenue for the local economy (p. 9 [153]).

        1.2.3.2.1. Pro:
        Many US entertainment and arts districts have been improved or expanded using tax-increment financing (p. 1 [154]).

        1.2.3.2.2. Con:
        Local employees in arts districts are likely to spend a significant amount of their income on entertainment in the area (p. 6 [155]).

        1.2.3.2.3. Pro:
        Americans regularly include art and culture-related activities in their vacations and often extend their trips [156] for cultural or arts-focused events.

      1.2.3.3. Pro:
      In France, the world's most popular country [157] for tourists, annual arts and culture funding [158] amounts to over €4bn.

        1.2.3.3.1. Con:
        Art is only a portion of what draws tourism to France. Skiing [159], vineyards [160], historical tours [161], and visits to popular cities [162] also contribute significantly to the tourist economy.

        1.2.3.3.2. Con:
        Arts and culture barely factor into plans for domestic French travelers, who number in the tens of millions and tend to stay away from Paris, instead preferring the coastal regions [163].

        1.2.3.3.3. Pro:
        The Louvre is the world's most popular museum and attracts over 10 million visitors [164] each year, 75% of whom are foreign visitors.

      1.2.3.4. Con:
      Tourism to many of the top destinations in the world is driven by natural beauty [165] instead of human-created arts and culture.

        1.2.3.4.1. Pro:
        Visiting national parks has become so popular that lodging rates have skyrocketed [166] over the last two decades—far outpacing hotel rates near centers of arts and culture.

        1.2.3.4.2. Con:
        City-nature combination trips are becoming more popular [167], suggesting that cities without strong cultural draws might miss out on tourism revenues.

    1.2.4. Con:
    Governments should focus on paying off their debt [168] instead of contributing funding to the arts.

      1.2.4.1. Con:
      Funding the arts and reducing government debt are not mutually exclusive goals.

        1.2.4.1.1. Pro:
        Funds can be redirected from other areas; for example, the amount of money spent on US military marching bands [169] is nearly double that of the federal arts budget, which pales in comparison to the defense budget overall [170].

        1.2.4.1.2. Con:
        Slashing the budget for non-essential spending, including arts programs, would allow the government to balance the budget without affecting essential services and programs (pp. 13, 15 [171]).

      1.2.4.2. Pro:
      Many European countries [172] continue to carry a debt-to-GDP ratio higher than the 60% cap established by the EU convergence criteria [173].

        1.2.4.2.1. Con:
        The cultural and creative sector contributes significantly to the GDP of many European countries and continues to grow (p. 3 [174]).

        1.2.4.2.2. Pro:
        In Europe, other industries, including tourism, hospitality, data analysis, information technology, AI, and robotics, are growing significantly faster [175] than the arts sector.

      1.2.4.3. Con:
      Funding the arts can help to reduce a country's debt.

        1.2.4.3.1. Pro:
        A healthy arts sector contributes substantial revenue [176] to the economy, providing a positive return with minimal government investment.

        1.2.4.3.2. Con:
        Many government-funded arts programs do not provide any financial return [177] to the nation.

      1.2.4.4. Pro:
      As of 2022, the US federal debt [178] was approximately $30.9 trillion dollars; there is an urgent need to allocate funds to reduce the amount of money the government owes.

        1.2.4.4.1. Pro:
        The US has reached its debt ceiling [179] and would be unable to support key programs if it became unable to borrow.

        1.2.4.4.2. Con:
        Annual funding for the National Endowment for the Arts (NEA) is US$201 million [180], which represents a minuscule portion of the US national budget—just 0.02% of government spending [181].

    1.2.5. Pro:
    Arts programs directly create economic activity via the sale of paintings, performance tickets, recordings, and other media.

      1.2.5.1. Pro:
      The arts industry in the US generates more than US$876 billion of economic activity [182] annually, equating to more than 4% of US GDP.

        1.2.5.1.1. Pro:
        It is normal for governments to subsidize essential industries [183], particularly those that generate significant revenue.

        1.2.5.1.2. Con:
        The government should not subsidize an industry of this size other than as a last resort [184], as this works contrary to the principles of a market economy [185].

          1.2.5.1.2.1. Con:
          Diversification of funding sources makes arts programs more financially resilient and better able to support their missions (p. 235 [186]).

      1.2.5.2. Con:
      Subsidies hurt the art industry [187] by leaving less room for the work of economically viable artists.

        1.2.5.2.1. Con:
        Many great artists, such as Vincent van Gogh [188], were not popular during their lifetime, and their work only became economically viable afterwards.

        1.2.5.2.2. Pro:
        Worthwhile art should be able to draw a large enough audience to be economically self-sustaining without government support.

          1.2.5.2.2.1. Con:
          Governments should provide funding for the arts, not for the sake of sustaining an arts audience, but for the benefits they provide to those who access the programs.

          1.2.5.2.2.2. Pro:
          People and private organizations will support works of art they appreciate and which resonate most with them; individuals instinctively realize whether a piece of art inspires, enlightens, entertains, or informs them.

      1.2.5.3. Pro:
      The arts and cultural activities account for more than 3% of France's GDP [189].

        1.2.5.3.1. Pro:
        Recorded media [190] contributes more revenue to the French economy than a few traditional industries [191].

        1.2.5.3.2. Con:
        Critics have identified high levels of state support as a contributor to the poor state of contemporary French visual art (p. 224 [192]).

  1.3. Pro:
  Arts programs are part of a vibrant civil society and, therefore, are an important part of any democracy (p. 2 [193]).

    1.3.1. Con:
    Democracy is in no way dependent upon arts programs for its establishment or operation.

      1.3.1.1. Pro:
      Arts programs are unnecessary [194] in any practical sense of the word.

        1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
        In the absence of government-funded arts programs, people who want to create or experience art could still freely do so.

        1.3.1.1.2. Con:
        Because arts programs have demonstrable benefits to society, it is irrelevant whether arts programs are necessary or not (p. 2 [195]).

      1.3.1.2. Con:
      Government-funded arts programs constitute avenues of public discourse that directly support the democratic process (p. 33 [196]).

        1.3.1.2.1. Pro:
        Works of art, including those produced by government-funded arts programs, can make original and relevant contributions to political discourse (pp. 560–561 [197]).

        1.3.1.2.2. Con:
        Art is, by its nature, imitative. As such, its ability to contribute meaningfully to real issues is limited, since it is inherently distinct from the realities it attempts to represent (p. 2 [198]).

      1.3.1.3. Con:
      Interaction with the arts has been shown to increase participation in the democratic process [199].

        1.3.1.3.1. Pro:
        The artistic process encourages mutualistic listening and expression [200], which are crucial elements of a successful democratic process.

        1.3.1.3.2. Con:
        The arts equally promote undemocratic forms of participation [201] by relying on artists and institutions to decide what is in the public's interest and dictate their tastes.

    1.3.2. Con:
    Government funding of arts programs undermines the independence of civil society [202] as a sector distinct from the government (pp. 10–11 [203]).

      1.3.2.1. Pro:
      The independence of private NGOs, including many arts programs, acts as an essential check on government power (p. 15 [204]).

        1.3.2.1.1. Pro:
        Arts programs and artists that receive government funds may be less motivated to be critical or hold the government accountable [205].

        1.3.2.1.2. Con:
        There is already sufficient oversight of the government by the media and NGOs [206] devoted specifically to this purpose. Arts programs do not need to fulfill this role.

      1.3.2.2. Con:
      The US government has made the support of civil society a national priority [207] and should continue to fund arts programs in keeping with this goal.

        1.3.2.2.1. Pro:
        Participation in arts programs is associated with increased civic engagement (p. 17 [208]).

        1.3.2.2.2. Con:
        It is easier for a government to control the activities of civil society organizations when they are reliant on government funds for their operation (p. 26 [209]).

      1.3.2.3. Pro:
      The funding of arts programs falls outside of the appropriate purview of the government (p. 181 [210]).

        1.3.2.3.1. Pro:
        The coercive nature of government power [211] should be reserved for defending rights and enforcing laws; the government has no business being involved in the arts.

        1.3.2.3.2. Con:
        Providing for the welfare and happiness of society is a primary function of the government (p. 3 [212]); as such, arts programs should be funded because they can contribute to the well-being of citizens [213].

      1.3.2.4. Con:
      The relationship between government and civil society in the modern world is complex and interdependent (p. 157 [214]).

        1.3.2.4.1. Pro:
        Civil society organizations must often rely heavily on developing positive relationships with government officials [215] in order to be effective.

        1.3.2.4.2. Con:
        Real or implied politicization is often used as a tactic to delegitimize the credibility of civil society [216] organizations; accepting government funds lends credence to this portrayal.

    1.3.3. Pro:
    Arts programs hold intrinsic social value [217] for their communities and society at large (p. 6 [218]).

      1.3.3.1. Pro:
      Cultural activities, such as those created by arts programs, are an essential component of human experience (p. 174 [219]).

        1.3.3.1.1. Pro:
        The Creative Ireland Program has worked to expand the role of the arts in daily life, highlighting the essential role of cultural activity [220].

        1.3.3.1.2. Con:
        People are likely to continue organizing and participating in cultural activities, as they have done throughout recorded history, regardless of whether they are funded by the government (p. 52 [221]).

      1.3.3.2. Con:
      The social value of art is based on the perception of the artists, audiences, and critics who choose whether to engage with it, and therefore it cannot be intrinsic (p. 11 [222]).

        1.3.3.2.1. Pro:
        Art holds different values for different groups; for example, galleries often instrumentally value art as a means of making a profit [223] rather than as an end unto itself.

        1.3.3.2.2. Con:
        Beauty and truth are central to art and are generally considered to be intrinsic values [224].

      1.3.3.3. Con:
      The social value of arts programs is manifestly instrumental [225]; those who stand to benefit from a particular arts program should be responsible for funding it.

        1.3.3.3.1. Pro:
        Arts programs are appreciated for the particular experiences, enjoyment, and meaning that they bring and are not an end unto themselves [226].

        1.3.3.3.2. Con:
        Art has inherent value as a medium of transmitting a highly organized consciousness which contributes to the growth and personal development of those consuming the art (p. 15 [227]).

      1.3.3.4. Pro:
      The Association of Art Museum Directors [228] has stated that it is "the mark of a great democracy to support the arts, which are an expression of what makes us human.”

        1.3.3.4.1. Con:
        Abiding by the will of the people with regard to arts funding, rather than indiscriminately supporting the arts, is the mark of a great democracy [229].

        1.3.3.4.2. Pro:
        In supporting the arts, nations acknowledge other narratives and perspectives [230] about their history; enabling such criticism is key for modern democracies.

    1.3.4. Pro:
    Arts programs provide society with culture, which is just as important as infrastructure [231].

      1.3.4.1. Con:
      Art does not provide culture; it is a product of a society's existing culture [232].

        1.3.4.1.1. Con:
        Art is essential to culture, as it contributes to the preservation, elaboration, and transmission of symbolic language [233].

        1.3.4.1.2. Pro:
        Art is one manifestation and representation [234] of a culture's beliefs, values, and experiences; culture encompasses art.

      1.3.4.2. Con:
      Arts funding predominantly benefits people living in cities, as rural areas are not well-known for their cultural and artistic contributions.

        1.3.4.2.1. Con:
        The NEA's Our Town [235] project supports the integration of local arts and culture into urban, suburban, rural, and tribal communities.

        1.3.4.2.2. Pro:
        Capital cities in the UK notoriously disproportionately benefit [236] from arts funding [237] compared with rural areas [238].

      1.3.4.3. Pro:
      Arts programs help to embody the values [239] of a society and create community identity (p. 1 [240]).

        1.3.4.3.1. Pro:
        The Mexican Muralism movement, sponsored largely by the post-revolutionary Mexican government, helped maintain fragile peace following the revolution, reuniting citizens and creating a sense of national and local pride [241].

        1.3.4.3.2. Con:
        A single community holds multiple, and sometimes conflicting, moral values [242]. Attempts to embody an entire group's values via arts programs can contribute to essentialism [243].

        1.3.4.3.3. Pro:
        In the postwar period, arts and culture programs helped define European national identities [244] and became an essential element of the modern state.

      1.3.4.4. Pro:
      Funding for the arts helps revitalize troubled neighborhoods [245].

        1.3.4.4.1. Pro:
        State-funded arts programs have proven to be a valuable element of revitalization in many areas (pp. 18, 23, 25 [246]).

        1.3.4.4.2. Con:
        Insofar as government funding is zero-sum, there are better ways to spend money on revitalizing neighborhoods, such as investing in private capital [247].

          1.3.4.4.2.1. Con:
          Government funding of arts programs and economic revitalization are not zero-sum [248]; both can occur simultaneously and bring benefits to the population at large.

        1.3.4.4.3. Con:
        The arts can also be a catalyst for gentrification [249].

          1.3.4.4.3.1. Pro:
          Research has identified an association between gentrification and the presence of local commercial arts clusters (pp. 16–17 [250]).

      1.3.4.5. Con:
      Infrastructure is more important than art, as it helps provide physiological necessities [251], which are more essential to human well-being than any benefits gleaned from art.

        1.3.4.5.1. Pro:
        People die from infrastructure deficiencies, such as the lack of access to safe drinking water [252], whereas nobody dies from a lack of art.

        1.3.4.5.2. Con:
        While physiological necessities are important for maintaining human life, this does not mean that the higher-level human needs [253] supported by art are less essential to the fulfillment of human potential and well-being.

    1.3.5. Pro:
    Art education programs teach people to communicate effectively across cultures and communities (pp. 103–104 [254]).

      1.3.5.1. Con:
      Art education programs can support divisive or controversial political ideologies [255] instead of promoting an unbiased perspective.

        1.3.5.1.1. Pro:
        Arts educators are more likely to hold liberal views and often fail to adequately address social issues from multiple perspectives [256].

        1.3.5.1.2. Con:
        This is an issue with education practices rather than with arts programs; socially engaged arts education is mindful of the broader context and diverse perspectives represented in the arts (pp. 59–60 [257]).

      1.3.5.2. Con:
      Most art education is predicated upon ethnocentric values and aesthetics and, therefore, may not be effective in teaching multicultural values (p. 216 [258]).

        1.3.5.2.1. Con:
        Multicultural art education can help to counteract ethnocentrism [259].

        1.3.5.2.2. Pro:
        White art critics devalue the artistic merit [260] of Black art, undermining the impact and outreach of Black artists.

      1.3.5.3. Pro:
      Art can be used as a medium through which cultural heritage and identity can be expressed and shared [261] by others around the world.

        1.3.5.3.1. Con:
        The art world is often associated with cultural hegemony, imposing rather than sharing cultural identity (p. 150 [262]).

        1.3.5.3.2. Pro:
        Federal funding for art installations has allowed many famous pieces of art from abroad to be shown in American cities [263].

      1.3.5.4. Pro:
      Art is a universal language [264]; people from different walks of life can appreciate and understand art without speaking the same language.

        1.3.5.4.1. Pro:
        Art can express complex ideas and emotions [265] that can be widely understood without reliance on written or spoken language.

        1.3.5.4.2. Con:
        The claim that art is a universal language is reductive, as it ignores the cultural context of art, upon which much of its meaning relies (pp. 1, 3 [266]).

    1.3.6. Con:
    Government funding unfairly forces [267] taxpayers to contribute to art that they would never engage with or do not approve of.

      1.3.6.1. Con:
      The representation of multiple perspectives in art supports the principles of free expression embodied in the First Amendment, which includes expressions of dissent (pp. 312–313 [268]).

        1.3.6.1.1. Con:
        The principles of free expression require society to allow expressions of dissent [269], not to financially support them.

        1.3.6.1.2. Pro:
        Art can often act as commentary on the issues faced by minority and oppressed communities [270]; support is essential for these groups to gain visibility and recognition.

      1.3.6.2. Pro:
      The government is not obligated to financially support every possible viewpoint; government funding of arts programs that lack public support constitutes taxation without representation [271] (p. 450 [272]).

        1.3.6.2.1. Con:
        Public opinion should not determine government funding for programs; oftentimes, government officials are well prepared to look out for the general public's best interests [273].

        1.3.6.2.2. Pro:
        Taxes should not be used to support arts programs without a method in place for voters to determine which programs will be funded.

      1.3.6.3. Pro:
      As the appreciation of art is inherently subjective [274], individuals should decide for themselves which arts programs they wish to support financially.

        1.3.6.3.1. Pro:
        Certain styles of art, such as Abstract Expressionism [275], are notoriously inaccessible to the public. Programs focused on such art should rely on its adherents for support.

        1.3.6.3.2. Con:
        The public might never be exposed to obscure or unknown genres [276] of art without government support [277] and publicity.

      1.3.6.4. Pro:
      Citizens may object to taxes being used to support arts programs that produce works containing offensive, indecent, or objectionable [278] content.

        1.3.6.4.1. Pro:
        The NEA subsidized the creation of Andres Serrano's "Immersion [279]," which some Christians find highly offensive.

        1.3.6.4.2. Pro:
        The NEA has subsidized [280] movies about oral sex, prostitution, restroom cruising, and the sexuality of children.

        1.3.6.4.3. Con:
        Controversial art [281] promotes social progress, since it challenges entrenched social norms and stimulates discussion [282] around taboo issues.

        1.3.6.4.4. Con:
        Governments can place restrictions on the funding [283] of art that is erotic or political in nature (p. 265 [284]).

      1.3.6.5. Con:
      Almost every individual engages with some form of art [285], be it through films, movies, or books. Therefore, they will inevitably receive some benefit from the government funding.

        1.3.6.5.1. Con:
        It is equally possible that individuals will encounter wasteful government-funded art from which they derive no benefit whatsoever [286].

        1.3.6.5.2. Pro:
        Some government-funded arts programs produce public artworks that are functional in nature and provide utility to the community (p. 2 [287]).

      1.3.6.6. Con:
      Government funding of the arts is a simple case of tax appropriation and affects taxpayers in the same way as other government-funded programs.

        1.3.6.6.1. Pro:
        The government often needs to use taxes to fund things that are not unanimously supported; for example, although some citizens may object to their taxes being used to support a particular military endeavor, funding for such programs is often mandatory [288].

        1.3.6.6.2. Con:
        Arts programs represent an entirely different category of tax spending, since they do not directly support the operation of the government or the welfare of the nation [289].

  1.4. Con:
  Government funding discourages charitable donations to arts programs (p. 1 [290]).

    1.4.1. Pro:
    When private donors become aware that a program is receiving government funding, they perceive less need and may become less likely to donate to it (pp. 3–4, 30 [291]).

      1.4.1.1. Pro:
      When government arts funding in the US was cut, private donors filled in the gap [292], and the total amount of funding for the arts increased.

        1.4.1.1.1. Con:
        Despite an increase in overall charitable giving in the US, donations to arts and culture decreased by 8.6% [293] in 2020.

        1.4.1.1.2. Pro:
        Private donors often join together to support [294] arts programs that the government has refused to fund.

      1.4.1.2. Con:
      The government does not fund the arts in its entirety, so there will always be a need for private donors.

        1.4.1.2.1. Con:
        The majority of governments do not fund any sector in its entirety, making this argument irrelevant.

        1.4.1.2.2. Pro:
        If the government were to fund the arts in its entirety, the amount of funding required would increase dramatically, as more people would work in the arts if they knew they were sure to receive funding.

    1.4.2. Con:
    Crowding out [295] of private donations by government spending occurs sparingly across a wide variety of arts sectors (p. 923 [296]).

      1.4.2.1. Con:
      Smaller orchestras experience a significant crowd-out of private donations with increased government funding (p. 17 [297]).

        1.4.2.1.1. Con:
        Different sources of private giving are not affected equally; smaller orchestras also experience crowd-in of business and foundation support with increased state funds (p. 18 [298]).

        1.4.2.1.2. Pro:
        This is consistent with the funding experience of nonprofit radio stations, wherein the amount of government grants are matched to the private donations received (p. 499 [299]).

      1.4.2.2. Pro:
      In many cases, what may initially appear as crowding out of private donations is actually a result of reduced crowding in at higher levels of public funding (p. 429 [300]).

        1.4.2.2.1. Pro:
        Low levels of public funding create the perception of need (p. 22 [301]); high levels of public funding demonstrate financial security.

        1.4.2.2.2. Con:
        Regardless of whether it is caused by crowd-out or a reduction in crowd-in, increasing public funding effectively lowers the ability of arts organizations to privately raise funds.

    1.4.3. Pro:
    Some nonprofit foundations require arts organizations to submit proof of their need for funding in order to receive a grant (p. 19 [302]).

      1.4.3.1. Con:
      If a particular arts program receives sufficient funding from the government and no longer requires needs-based funding, then the nonprofit funds can easily be directed to other programs that are still in need.

        1.4.3.1.1. Pro:
        Nonprofit organizations have predetermined budgets [303]; they can easily decide to fund different arts programs within the scope of their mission [304].

        1.4.3.1.2. Con:
        Most budgetary decisions at both government agencies and nonprofits are made during the previous fiscal year, so funds cannot be quickly shifted to alternative programs in need (p. 4 [305]).

      1.4.3.2. Pro:
      Accepting government funds could lead to arts programs becoming ineligible for needs-based funds, thereby decreasing the total amount of funding they receive.

        1.4.3.2.1. Con:
        Such a situation would likely be addressed by further rounds of funding since government arts grants generally include an assessment [306] of the funding's impact [307].

        1.4.3.2.2. Pro:
        The average NEA grant is more than US$30,000 [308], which is substantial enough to affect eligibility for needs-based funds.

      1.4.3.3. Con:
      Having multiple methods of funding arts organizations is useful; government and nonprofit funding follow different criteria, which promotes diversity among the programs.

        1.4.3.3.1. Pro:
        Arts programs that are unable to fund themselves can often find private funding when public funding is unavailable (p. 3 [309]).

        1.4.3.3.2. Con:
        All arts programs should be held to the same standards and criteria when seeking financial support.

      1.4.3.4. Pro:
      Nonprofit, needs-based funding is a better method of supporting arts programs than government funding.

        1.4.3.4.1. Con:
        Nonprofit funders can often hold unrealistic expectations of grantees, who can be forced to acquiesce when they are desperate for funding (p. 170 [310]).

        1.4.3.4.2. Pro:
        Needs-based funding provides struggling programs with crucial support and prevents programs with deep pockets from getting excessive funding.

    1.4.4. Con:
    Tax incentives that subsidize charitable giving [311] are more wasteful and inefficient than directly funding the arts.

      1.4.4.1. Con:
      Tax incentives [312] on donations to arts programs act as significant incentives for private charitable giving.

        1.4.4.1.1. Pro:
        Brazilian and international corporations that voluntarily fund movies, theater productions, art exhibitions, and museums receive significant tax breaks [313] from the Brazilian government (p. 59 [314]).

        1.4.4.1.2. Con:
        Not everyone donates to the arts [315] for a tax break; some people simply want to support sources [316] of personal entertainment and joy.

        1.4.4.1.3. Pro:
        The charitable tax deduction [317] directly promotes philanthropic support of the arts, with most individuals who donate to the arts doing so for philanthropic [318] motives.

      1.4.4.2. Pro:
      Tax incentives [319] deprive the government of revenue which could be used to help fund other industries.

        1.4.4.2.1. Pro:
        Billionaires like Warren Buffet use tax incentives to donate to charities and nonprofit organizations because they believe that they are better at allocating [320] their money than the federal government.

        1.4.4.2.2. Con:
        The financial support generated by tax incentives on donations to arts programs is greater than the amount of lost tax revenue (p. 15 [321]).

    1.4.5. Con:
    In response to decreased government funding of the arts, many private charitable organizations have maintained or lowered the amount they give to arts programs (p. 15 [322]).

      1.4.5.1. Pro:
      Government funding can act as a sign of legitimacy for arts programs; private donors may be reluctant to support unqualified programs (p. 10 [323]).

        1.4.5.1.1. Pro:
        Charitable donors may be concerned about the legal implications of donating to struggling arts programs [324] since the intent of their donation could easily be ignored if the program goes bankrupt.

          1.4.5.1.1.1. Pro:
          Many donors give funds to arts organizations on the provision that the use of the funds be restricted to a particular purpose (p. 1 [325]).

          1.4.5.1.1.2. Con:
          Without donations, these struggling arts programs will certainly go bankrupt. Patrons of the arts would likely prefer to attempt to help preserve these programs, as opposed to doing nothing at all.

        1.4.5.1.2. Con:
        The government cannot not be held responsible for the funding decisions of private donors.

          1.4.5.1.2.1. Con:
          The impact of government funding on private donations is well documented, so the government would be remiss in not considering the downstream effects of its funding decisions (p. 301 [326]).

          1.4.5.1.2.2. Pro:
          The government cannot be expected to continue funding arts programs based on the possible behavior of private donors. If the public is concerned about the effects of funding cuts, then they can donate on their own or help contribute to fundraising efforts.

      1.4.5.2. Con:
      The study referenced in the parent claim was performed during the 2008 recession, which easily accounts for the decline in private funding (p. 2 [327]).

        1.4.5.2.1. Pro:
        The relationship between government arts funding and private donations is complex; accurately modelling this relationship requires consideration of numerous related variables (p. 22 [328]).

        1.4.5.2.2. Con:
        Private donations are directly affected by political decisions; policy discussions affect public priorities and awareness [329] and impact the focus of private giving.

    1.4.6. Pro:
    Private organizations [330] and individuals are the primary source of funding for many arts programs, so they cannot afford to discourage such donations.

      1.4.6.1. Con:
      Government funding institutions, like the National Endowment for the Arts, were created to address disparities and biases [331] in private funding.

        1.4.6.1.1. Pro:
        Minority and community-based arts programs have been chronically underfunded by private funding and rely heavily on government funding for their continued existence (p. 16 [332]).

          1.4.6.1.1.1. Pro:
          The NEA Equity Action Plan focuses on improving both access and participation in arts programs for underserved communities (p. 3 [333]).

          1.4.6.1.1.2. Con:
          Private donors fund the projects they deem worthwhile. If the market does not view such community-based arts programs as valuable, perhaps they do not need to be funded.

        1.4.6.1.2. Pro:
        Smaller arts programs receive substantially less funding from private sources, yet they serve a significantly larger aggregate audience (p. 24 [334]).

        1.4.6.1.3. Con:
        Rather than directly addressing such disparities with program funds, government funding could be employed on a limited basis to support awareness of such disparities and drive increased private contributions.

        1.4.6.1.4. Con:
        Private funding institutions also address bias in arts funding; private organizations, such as Grantmakers in the Arts [335] (GIA), have been founded expressly for this purpose.

      1.4.6.2. Pro:
      Private citizens contribute more than US$10 billion a year [336] to the arts, culture, and humanities in the US.

        1.4.6.2.1. Con:
        The government is well positioned to identify gaps and oversight in private support of the arts and to address them with targeted funding programs.

          1.4.6.2.1.1. Con:
          Government funding for museums is overwhelmingly biased in favor of large, well-known museums [337] over small, independent ones, who have had to seek funding [338] from other sources.

        1.4.6.2.2. Pro:
        Crowdfunding platforms, such as Kickstarter [339], have made it easier for private donors to directly contribute to the arts programs they personally endorse.

      1.4.6.3. Pro:
      Businesses are a significant source of charitable donations [340] to arts programs as a form of community investment.

        1.4.6.3.1. Pro:
        There are significant opportunities for businesses and arts programs to create mutually beneficial [341] partnerships and collaborations without relying on government funds.

        1.4.6.3.2. Con:
        Business donations to the arts that are intended to support community development are less likely to be affected by government funding since they are driven by opportunity rather than need (p. 4 [342]).

      1.4.6.4. Con:
      It is worse for society if arts programs are funded by private organizations, since funding from certain companies, such as BP [343], comes from potentially immoral sources.

        1.4.6.4.1. Pro:
        Some corporations consider the sponsorship of art programs to be a form of PR, which can be used to whitewash [344] unethical business or environmental practices (p. 146 [345]).

        1.4.6.4.2. Con:
        Diversity initiatives [346] have impacted the grant and funding processes of many businesses, so corporate donations are increasingly likely to support underrepresented groups and perspectives.

  1.5. Con:
  Government funding of arts programs will inevitably lead to increased government influence [347] and control over art.

    1.5.1. Pro:
    Government funding of arts programs is associated with a great deal of inefficiency and administrative overhead (p. 4 [348]).

      1.5.1.1. Con:
      This is an argument for increasing the efficiency of the government funding process, not an argument against funding arts programs.

        1.5.1.1.1. Pro:
        Many essential government-funded programs have high overhead and bureaucratic costs (p. 1 [349]); the arts should not be punished for government inefficiency.

        1.5.1.1.2. Con:
        A number of the programs funded by the NEA have used significant funds for frivolous and wasteful [350] purposes.

      1.5.1.2. Con:
      Nearly all government-funded programs around the world are rife with waste and inefficiency [351]. Arts programs are not unique in this regard.

        1.5.1.2.1. Con:
        While inefficiency in socially necessary government programs is an unfortunate reality, waste is entirely unacceptable for nonessential luxuries [352], such as arts programs.

        1.5.1.2.2. Pro:
        Since the amount of government funds devoted to arts programs is tiny in comparison with other spending, efforts to reduce waste and inefficiency should be focused on larger spending categories [353] where the impact will be greater.

      1.5.1.3. Pro:
      When the arts receive government funding, the cost [354] of production often increases, rather than the quantity of artistic pieces.

        1.5.1.3.1. Pro:
        Government funding reduces the accountability of art [355] to popular, and therefore commercial, success.

        1.5.1.3.2. Con:
        In the absence of government funding, numerous galleries would be forced to close [356], significantly decreasing public access to art.

      1.5.1.4. Pro:
      Privatization [357] of arts funding would result in a more efficient system.

        1.5.1.4.1. Pro:
        Government funding in general creates waste and complacency [358].

        1.5.1.4.2. Con:
        Private nonprofit organizations are often an equally significant source of bureaucratic overhead and wasteful administrative costs [359].

    1.5.2. Pro:
    Government funding of art forces politicians to justify the spending, which leads to ideological problems.

      1.5.2.1. Con:
      Art is inherently political [360] whether or not the government funds it.

        1.5.2.1.1. Con:
        A great deal of art is primarily concerned with aesthetic experience and personal expression [361] rather than representation.

        1.5.2.1.2. Pro:
        Art inherently treats particular values, perspectives, and subjects [362] as important, which carries political ramifications.

        1.5.2.1.3. Con:
        Not all art is political [363]. Describing it as such dilutes the significance of the word "political."

        1.5.2.1.4. Pro:
        Avoiding political issues in art is effectively a decision to support the status quo [364].

      1.5.2.2. Pro:
      Government spending should be reasonable and easily justifiable [365], but evaluating arts programs this way is extremely difficult.

        1.5.2.2.1. Pro:
        The primary justifications for public arts funding center on aesthetic and moral values [366], which are difficult to address and exclude certain forms of art.

        1.5.2.2.2. Con:
        The purpose of government is to maximize the happiness [367] of citizens; since exposure to and engagement with art improves psychological well-being, it is a valid use of government funds (p. 2 [368]).

        1.5.2.2.3. Pro:
        Appropriations bills must define what constitutes art [369] in order to authorize funding.

          1.5.2.2.3.1. Con:
          Appropriations bills could use a broad definition [370] that includes many different types of art, including its most traditional forms [371].

          1.5.2.2.3.2. Pro:
          Art is notoriously difficult to define [372], as it relies on relative concepts, such as beauty or the importance of ideas, and is therefore highly subject to personal interpretation (p. 109 [373]); some have argued that it is impossible to arrive at a meaningful definition that is not exclusionary.

          1.5.2.2.3.3. Con:
          In the US, government funds are appropriated en masse to the NEA by the legislature, where panels of experts and professionals with a wide range of artistic perspectives [374] determine which projects to fund. Thus, the NEA rather than the legislature defines what constitutes art.

        1.5.2.2.4. Con:
        The arts have a positive impact on mental health [375] during difficult times, and cutting funding can be detrimental to well-being. As such, government funding of arts programs can be considered an investment [376] in improving mental health.

    1.5.3. Con:
    Decentralized arts funding, as employed by the NEA, ensures that no single agency has control over the arts programs that are funded (pp. viii, 3 [377]).

      1.5.3.1. Con:
      Funding for the NEA remains subject to cultural and political agendas (p. 181 [378]).

        1.5.3.1.1. Pro:
        At times, the NEA has encouraged funded artists to promote the agendas of politicians [379], such as President Obama.

        1.5.3.1.2. Con:
        The primary review criteria [380] for NEA grants are artistic excellence and artistic merit rather than political concerns.

      1.5.3.2. Con:
      Even in cases where arts funding is decentralized, governments can still censor [381] art that is politically motivated or potentially objectionable by defunding [382] it.

        1.5.3.2.1. Pro:
        Political art is a crucial form of activism [383] and should therefore not be subject to any government control.

          1.5.3.2.1.1. Pro:
          Paintings made with menstrual blood have helped to challenge societal norms in India [384].

          1.5.3.2.1.2. Con:
          Those who are engaging in impactful activism are less likely to seek state funding, since they may consider the government unlikely to support subversive or challenging art.

          1.5.3.2.1.3. Con:
          Art cannot be meaningfully used as a mechanism for activism [385].

          1.5.3.2.1.4. Pro:
          Artists [386] like Pussy Riot and Banksy create subversive art that challenges the government and the status quo.

        1.5.3.2.2. Pro:
        The government is unlikely to fund publicly displayed art that can be seen as derogatory or defamatory, as a way to keep things civil.

          1.5.3.2.2.1. Pro:
          Government-funded arts programs often refuse to fund art that is not politically correct [387].

          1.5.3.2.2.2. Con:
          Outcry from government officials over the content and themes of publicly funded art has had little effect [388] on what can or cannot be produced.

        1.5.3.2.3. Con:
        Artists remain free to create art within the confines of their country's rules on personal expression; in the US, artistic expression can only be curtailed when it fulfills specific, defined criteria [389].

      1.5.3.3. Pro:
      US arts funding is included in the president's yearly budget and enacted by the legislature through appropriation bills. However, the funds are distributed to individual programs by the NEA (p. 3 [390]).

        1.5.3.3.1. Con:
        There is still a selection process for NEA panelists [391], so the funding process remains subject to political motivations.

        1.5.3.3.2. Pro:
        The NEA is required to distribute 40% of its budget to local and state-level arts councils [392].

      1.5.3.4. Pro:
      If different groups are responsible for deciding the appropriations for arts programs and choosing which programs and artists will be funded, then government influence over art is reduced.

        1.5.3.4.1. Pro:
        Under such a system, government funding can support more experimental styles of art [393] or art that opposes government values and ideals.

        1.5.3.4.2. Con:
        While the funds for individual projects are awarded locally, a reliance on NEA funds places arts programs and artists at the mercy of the president and Congress, who can decide to withhold funds [394] from the arts if they so choose.

    1.5.4. Pro:
    When those making the funding decisions [395] can determine which arts programs receive support, government funding is likely to unfairly favor specific artistic styles, statements, and artists.

      1.5.4.1. Con:
      Funding via any method with the exception of a blinded process will come with implicit or explicit restrictions.

        1.5.4.1.1. Con:
        There is a difference between individual citizens and organizations choosing to fund arts programs they support and state representatives placing paternalistic restrictions on particular types of art (p. 15 [396]).

        1.5.4.1.2. Pro:
        If art is only privately funded, groups and individuals [397] will be able to incentivize certain types of art, limiting the options to those they find interesting or appropriate.

      1.5.4.2. Pro:
      Some art forms disproportionately benefit [398] from long-term government funding. This obstructs the funding of newer and more innovative art start-ups.

        1.5.4.2.1. Con:
        Some forms of art are more well established and traditional [399]; it is natural for programs focused on these forms to receive greater funding than experimental forms.

        1.5.4.2.2. Pro:
        In the UK, over 60% of arts funding [400] goes towards opera (often considered a niche and repetitive [401] art form), while only 1% is set aside for other genres, such as community choirs and bands.

        1.5.4.2.3. Pro:
        Artists and styles outside the mainstream are often marginalized by a lack of funding (p. 8 [402]).

          1.5.4.2.3.1. Con:
          Marginalized art is not necessarily a bad thing; marginalization has inspired some of the world's best artists [403].

      1.5.4.3. Pro:
      When governments fund art, they often use it to commission propaganda [404], in the form of art, that engenders a positive image of the government.

        1.5.4.3.1. Pro:
        Governments already spend large amounts of money on spin and public relations [405] without much public outcry.

          1.5.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          US intelligence and military agencies, such as the OWI [406] and the CIA [407], have often partnered with Hollywood to make films and have often influenced how the US and its military are portrayed.

          1.5.4.3.1.2. Con:
          Explicit government funding of public relations is less concerning than the funding of propaganda under the guise of support for the arts.

        1.5.4.3.2. Pro:
        Government regimes have historically commissioned the creation of posters [408] and films [409] to influence the public and further their cause (p. 1 [410]).

        1.5.4.3.3. Con:
        Many pieces of counter-propaganda [411] work have been created in response to government-funded artwork.

          1.5.4.3.3.1. Pro:
          During the Cold War, the CIA subsidized the works of American Abstract Expressionists [412] as a cultural counterweight to Soviet Realist artwork.

        1.5.4.3.4. Con:
        Despite widespread connotations, not all propaganda is negative; it can also be used to fuel accepted positive agendas [413].

          1.5.4.3.4.1. Pro:
          Propaganda has served positive purposes, including garnering support for the American Revolution [414], supporting WWII war efforts [415], and attempting to inspire social change [416].

          1.5.4.3.4.2. Pro:
          Many positive public health initiatives [417] have been supported by the use of propaganda.

          1.5.4.3.4.3. Con:
          Perspectives on propaganda are subjective; those opposing the accepted views are unlikely to view such propaganda in a positive light.

      1.5.4.4. Con:
      The artistic styles supported by government funding reflect the ideals and desires of the public via their elected representatives.

        1.5.4.4.1. Pro:
        Decisions about government arts funding in the UK follow a decidedly political pattern and are largely driven by electoral concerns (p. 341 [418]).

        1.5.4.4.2. Con:
        Government funding of arts programs suffers from the principal-agent problem, as politicians are often of a different socioeconomic class than their constituency (pp. 2–3, 26 [419]).

    1.5.5. Con:
    The same case could be made regarding scientific funding, but increased government influence is not a valid reason for denying funds to scientific programs.

      1.5.5.1. Pro:
      Similar to the arts, the majority of funding for scientific research comes from private industry, so the influence of the government is limited (p. 156 [420]).

        1.5.5.1.1. Pro:
        Private funding is usually associated with fewer regulations [421] than government funding.

        1.5.5.1.2. Con:
        The US government provides more than US$28 billion annually to fund academic research and development (p. 1 [422]).

      1.5.5.2. Pro:
      Government-sponsored studies tend to be less biased [423] than privately funded research sponsored by corporations; the same pattern may apply to the arts as well.

        1.5.5.2.1. Pro:
        Corporate funding is invariably tied to a company's financial interests (p. 141 [424]).

        1.5.5.2.2. Con:
        The arts are not subject to the same checks on bias as scientific research; for example, artwork is not peer reviewed before publication.

      1.5.5.3. Con:
      Government funding subjects scientific research to political agendas, which can have significant effects on scientific progress (pp. 7–8 [425]).

        1.5.5.3.1. Pro:
        Government-funded arts programs are similarly subject to political agendas, which can greatly affect artistic intent (p. 227, 230–231 [426]).

        1.5.5.3.2. Con:
        Artistic achievement is already subject to the political agendas of individual artists and art institutions.

      1.5.5.4. Con:
      Conflict of interest is a serious concern in scientific research and can constitute a valid reason for withholding funding (p. 7 [427]).

        1.5.5.4.1. Pro:
        Arts programs should similarly be required to identify their funding sources and disclose any conflicts this creates.

        1.5.5.4.2. Con:
