        Conflicts of interest in scientific research and art are distinctly different; an artistic conflict of interest does not necessarily detract from the value of the work.

    1.5.6. Con:
    The ability of a government to censor [428] art exists irrespective of whether the government funds art.

      1.5.6.1. Pro:
      The possibility of government censorship is not a valid reason for defunding arts programs, as this would essentially constitute censorship of an even wider range of artists and styles.

        1.5.6.1.1. Pro:
        Many government-funded arts programs continue to operate successfully without concerns about government censorship [429].

        1.5.6.1.2. Con:
        With increasing government involvement, many artists and institutions begin self-censoring [430]; this affects all art, not only that which is funded by the state.

      1.5.6.2. Con:
      Government censorship of art is generally limited [431], and private entities are unable to restrict the type of art that can be created.

        1.5.6.2.1. Con:
        Although the more serious forms of censorship are rare in most countries, music remains disproportionately censored [432] compared with visual art forms.

        1.5.6.2.2. Pro:
        While artistic freedom is not absolute, most modern states [433] support the right of artists to free expression.

      1.5.6.3. Con:
      In the US, the government is not allowed to censor art that they have decided to fund, as per a Supreme Court [434] ruling.

        1.5.6.3.1. Pro:
        The US government remains obligated to follow the principles of the First Amendment [435] whether or not art is publicly funded.

        1.5.6.3.2. Con:
        Government-funded art in the US is subject to review and defunding if it is found to be indecent or obscene [436], which is a highly personal and ill-defined measure.

      1.5.6.4. Pro:
      In many countries, artists face significant threats to their artistic freedom through censorship, imprisonment, physical threats, and killings (p. 4 [437]).

        1.5.6.4.1. Pro:
        State-sponsored censorship often seeks to prevent the dissemination of messages that are deemed undesirable [438] through any means, including art.

        1.5.6.4.2. Con:
        Artists are typically censored for making political statements [439] rather than for simply creating art, so they should accept that addressing political topics will inevitably have political consequences [440].

  1.6. Con:
  There is little evidence that government funding for arts programs is helpful in qualitatively or quantitatively expanding the arts.

    1.6.1. Pro:
    When art is privately—rather than publicly—funded, artists are motivated to create better art with greater commercial appeal to align with the desires of consumers.

      1.6.1.1. Pro:
      Art is a commercial product; therefore, good art is more responsive to the desires of consumers and the market (pp. 24, 26 [441]).

        1.6.1.1.1. Con:
        The commercial value of artwork is of lesser concern than its unique artistic value, which is paramount (p. 359 [442]).

        1.6.1.1.2. Pro:
        Art is inherently a product [443] since it has monetary value.

      1.6.1.2. Pro:
      The art world is not exempt from the economic realities faced by other industries; art that ignores commercial exposure is out of touch (p. 3 [444]).

        1.6.1.2.1. Pro:
        The art market is increasingly moving to a consumer-driven model [445].

          1.6.1.2.1.1. Pro:
          Digital art can be created for consumers with specific needs or interests [446] – more quickly and easily now than in the past.

          1.6.1.2.1.2. Con:
          Some art markets [447] have become increasingly driven by the artists [448]. Government support is meant to allow artists to express themselves as they feel, not as consumers demand.

        1.6.1.2.2. Con:
        Some forms of art, such as digital art and NFTs, can allow artists to more easily take ownership of their art and continue profiting [449] from it with each subsequent sale.

        1.6.1.2.3. Con:
        When art is considered a product, artists have to cater their art towards the masses rather than following their artistic vision [450].

          1.6.1.2.3.1. Pro:
          Many artists create art in line with their personal vision and are more concerned with the art itself [451] than with selling it; government funding enables such artists to produce art for art's sake.

          1.6.1.2.3.2. Con:
          Artists only need their art to resonate with an audience that is large enough to support their efforts; catering to the masses is not necessary.

      1.6.1.3. Con:
      The quality and value of art are not based on popularity or sales.

        1.6.1.3.1. Pro:
        Art holds aesthetic value [452] independent of its financial value.

        1.6.1.3.2. Pro:
        Since the appreciation of art is inherently [453] subjective, works of art can be incredibly impactful for a small number of people.

        1.6.1.3.3. Con:
        The value of art is determined by what people are willing to pay for it according to the laws of supply and demand.

      1.6.1.4. Con:
      If artists must create art with broad appeal in order to make money, then they will tend to create art that is inoffensive and pushes no boundaries.

        1.6.1.4.1. Pro:
        The importance of many iconic works of art is based upon their controversial nature [454].

        1.6.1.4.2. Con:
        Artists are perfectly capable of producing controversial or offensive art that has mass appeal. For example, Swiss artist HR Giger's art was both widely popular and shocking [455] to many, and was censored [456] at times despite being accepted in popular culture [457].

        1.6.1.4.3. Con:
        Broad appeal and commercial success mean nothing to artists who dislike being told what to do [458] by their clientele.

    1.6.2. Pro:
    Government funding promotes mediocrity in the arts (p. 9 [459]).

      1.6.2.1. Con:
      A lack of government funding for the arts would decrease the time, training, and resources available to artists, resulting in the production of fewer or lower-quality works.

        1.6.2.1.1. Pro:
        Some media and projects require time, training, or funding that are unavailable to many artists without government funding.

          1.6.2.1.1.1. Con:
          Independent artists can seek funding from private donors and family members or use their earnings from a side job.

          1.6.2.1.1.2. Pro:
          An independent first film requires a minimum budget of at least US$10,000–$25,000 [460].

        1.6.2.1.2. Pro:
        The quality of art is highly subjective; many audiences expect qualities that require additional time, resources, and funds [461] to achieve.

        1.6.2.1.3. Con:
        Private funding for the arts has a much larger impact [462] than government funding; quality artists have relied on themselves and the private sector in the past and would continue to do so in the future even without government funding.

        1.6.2.1.4. Con:
        Many outstanding modern works of art [463] have been produced entirely without government funds.

      1.6.2.2. Pro:
      Government support of arts programs is often driven by reasons distinct from the quality of art, such as cultural or historical concerns, which can lead to the promotion of mediocre works [464].

        1.6.2.2.1. Con:
        Government-funded arts programs have helped to preserve and promote indigenous art forms [465] that might otherwise be lost.

        1.6.2.2.2. Pro:
        A great deal of the art in museums holds historical [466] rather than artistic value.

      1.6.2.3. Con:
      The overwhelming majority of art is mediocre [467] regardless of whether or not it is funded by the government.

        1.6.2.3.1. Pro:
        Even renowned artistic geniuses, including Michelangelo, Rembrandt, Goya, and Van Gogh, produced a number of mediocre works [468].

        1.6.2.3.2. Con:
        There is no such thing as good or bad art [469], as all art is equally an expression of the artist's feelings and perspective.

          1.6.2.3.2.1. Con:
          The quality of art can be critically evaluated in a way that accounts for the intentions of the artist [470].

      1.6.2.4. Con:
      Any program that supports the creation of art will ultimately lead to improvement over time; a lack of support is more likely to promote mediocrity.

        1.6.2.4.1. Pro:
        Government funding promotes the development and maintenance of artistic discourse communities [471], which improve the quality of art that is produced.

        1.6.2.4.2. Con:
        Some arts organizations, such as the Museum of Bad Art (MOBA [472]), directly promote avowedly mediocre or bad art.

      1.6.2.5. Pro:
      Government funding of arts programs allows average artists who are less technically proficient [473] to become financially successful.

        1.6.2.5.1. Pro:
        Artists who receive adequate income from government funding may become complacent with their current skills rather than pushing their artistic boundaries further.

        1.6.2.5.2. Con:
        A modicum of financial success [474] supports the development of more skilled artists, as it enables them to focus on cultivating their art.

      1.6.2.6. Pro:
      Since some of the best art in the world has been produced under duress or difficult circumstance [475], the quality of art made without government funding will be higher.

        1.6.2.6.1. Pro:
        Sadness, pain, and difficult circumstances can be catalysts for increased self-awareness [476], which can result in more profound and meaningful works of art.

        1.6.2.6.2. Con:
        It is a myth [477] that art made under difficult circumstances is of a higher quality.

    1.6.3. Con:
    There are numerous government-funded arts programs that have clearly aided in the creation of meaningful art or expanded access to such artistic works.

      1.6.3.1. Pro:
      The US government has funded successful arts programs in the past and continues to do so.

        1.6.3.1.1. Pro:
        The NEA funds a diverse range of over 2,000 arts programs and studies the impact of programs they support (p. 3 [478]).

        1.6.3.1.2. Con:
        In many cases, US government funding of arts endeavors, such as the Treasury Section of Painting and Sculpture [479], has proven to be unsuccessful. There is no reason to believe that the government will eliminate wasteful spending on failing projects.

        1.6.3.1.3. Pro:
        During the Great Depression, the Public Works of Art Project [480] (PWAP) and its successor, the Federal Art Project [481] (FAP), helped revitalize America's art culture and resulted in the creation of many influential works.

          1.6.3.1.3.1. Pro:
          The WPA/FAP supported a number of renowned artists [482], including Jackson Pollock and Mark Rothko, as they honed their craft.

          1.6.3.1.3.2. Con:
          The government funding of public works of art in the New Deal did not result in the creation of any true masterpieces [483].

            1.6.3.1.3.2.1. Con:
            A number of artists, including Sargent Claude Johnson [484] and Diego Rivera [485], are considered to have created masterpieces under the auspices of the Federal Art Project [486] (FAP).

            1.6.3.1.3.2.2. Pro:
            Given the lack of enduring and influential works created by New Deal art programs, the money spent on public works of art could be considered a waste.

              1.6.3.1.3.2.2.1. Con:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.3.

            1.6.3.1.3.2.3. Pro:
            The majority of the artists supported by the program were under the age of 40 [487], by which point most artists have either garnered success at their craft or switched to another occupation. As such, the names of most of the artists are not well recognized.

      1.6.3.2. Pro:
      The Swedish Arts Grants Committee [488] is a government agency tasked with promoting and supporting the creation of art.

        1.6.3.2.1. Pro:
        The Swedish Arts Grants Committee works with both domestic and foreign artists, promoting internationalization [489] and cultural exchange.

        1.6.3.2.2. Con:
        More than one-third of artists in Sweden report being subject to harassment and threats, often resulting in self-censorship (p. 268 [490]). Arts funding is irrelevant if artists cannot create freely.

      1.6.3.3. Con:
      While many countries have reduced the amount of government funds [491] devoted to arts programs, there has not been a lack of art being produced.

        1.6.3.3.1. Con:
        A sufficient amount of art being produced does not imply that the art is of high quality. A great deal of contemporary art is meaningless to most people [492].

        1.6.3.3.2. Pro:
        Artists are driven to create [493] and will continue to do so whether they receive government funding or not.

    1.6.4. Con:
    Many arts programs would be unable to continue operating without support from the government.

      1.6.4.1. Con:
      There are many successful self-funded [494] arts programs.

        1.6.4.1.1. Pro:
        Self-funded arts programs allow artists to focus on their personal visions and priorities [495] without worrying about the influence of outside stakeholders.

        1.6.4.1.2. Con:
        Self-funding often requires artists to work in other fields to support their own art [496]; this option may not be available for those without significant disposable income.

      1.6.4.2. Con:
      Arts programs that receive government funding have an unfair advantage over unfunded programs.

        1.6.4.2.1. Con:
        Expensive, unfunded art caters to wealthy individuals. Government funding provides countless opportunities to impoverished citizens.

        1.6.4.2.2. Pro:
        Government funding unfairly benefits more experienced programs, who are already familiar with the application and review processes (p. 14 [497]).

      1.6.4.3. Con:
      Government support makes up a small portion [498] of funding for most arts programs.

        1.6.4.3.1. Con:
        Literary organizations [499] rely heavily on government support in comparison with other types of arts programs.

        1.6.4.3.2. Pro:
        For every dollar of NEA funding, arts programs raise nine dollars [500] of funding from other sources.

      1.6.4.4. Pro:
      Many artists could not afford to choose art as a career [501] without government funding.

        1.6.4.4.1. Pro:
        Without government funding [502] for the arts, talented artists may be forced to quit producing art entirely in order to make a living [503].

        1.6.4.4.2. Con:
        While some governments support artists' careers, this approach to state funding of the arts is rare; in countries like the UK, where funding for the arts [504] pales in comparison, nearly every artist is supported by themselves and/or private funding.

        1.6.4.4.3. Pro:
        A number of the world's most famous artists struggled while they were alive [505] and only garnered acclaim after their death—government funding and support would have helped them live a more comfortable life.

        1.6.4.4.4. Con:
        Nepotism [506] is already rife in the arts, and there is no reason to believe that government funding will be directed towards those who could not otherwise afford to make art.

        1.6.4.4.5. Pro:
        Artists deserve to be compensated for their work. With the exception of a minority of high-visibility household names, they are almost universally undervalued [507] by the free market.

          1.6.4.4.5.1. Pro:
          In general, an increase in the amount of effort required [508] to produce a product increases its value.

          1.6.4.4.5.2. Con:
          The economic value [509] of a product is based on the benefits it brings rather than the work required to make it.

      1.6.4.5. Pro:
      Without the subsidization of arts programs [510], up to 30,000 art exhibitions and music, theater, and dance performance venues could disappear.

        1.6.4.5.1. Con:
        If there is truly a market for these programs and services, then new venues more aligned with the audience's desires will open up to meet the need.

        1.6.4.5.2. Pro:
        Government funding [511] is essential for museums around the world [512], which would otherwise likely have to close.

      1.6.4.6. Pro:
      During the COVID-19 pandemic, government funding [513] was used to subsidize a wide range of arts organizations [514] to counter the lack of investment caused by the outbreak.

        1.6.4.6.1. Con:
        Government funding for the arts during the COVID-19 pandemic was convoluted, difficult to apply for, and inaccessible for most businesses [515] in the arts industry.

        1.6.4.6.2. Pro:
        The 2021 American Rescue Plan [516] included an additional US$135 million allocation for supporting the arts.

        1.6.4.6.3. Pro:
        Art is affected by the decisions [517] of politicians under the status quo, such as whether or not to close public buildings during the COVID-19 pandemic.

        1.6.4.6.4. Con:
        The primary purpose of COVID-19 subsidies to arts organizations was maintaining jobs rather than supporting the arts (p. 1 [518]).

          1.6.4.6.4.1. Con:
          Maintaining jobs in the arts industry does support the arts. Without access to secure income during the pandemic, artists likely would have moved on to other endeavors, and the arts industry would have suffered from a lack of available workers similar to the 2022 aviation industry labor shortage [519].

          1.6.4.6.4.2. Pro:
          The Shuttered Venue Operators Grant (SVOG [520]) program allowed Broadway shows to continue operating through the pandemic despite the lack of audiences.

            1.6.4.6.4.2.1. Pro:
            Broadway is one of the most popular tourist destinations in New York and generates nearly US$15 billion [521] in annual revenue; losing this attraction would have been ruinous to the economy.

            1.6.4.6.4.2.2. Pro:
            Broadway supports nearly 100,000 jobs [522] in New York City, the vast majority of which would have been lost without government funding during the COVID-19 pandemic.

    1.6.5. Con:
    Public funds frequently catalyze [523] increased private investment.

      1.6.5.1. Pro:
      Increased private investment is associated with growth across a wide range of industries (p. 1 [524]).

        1.6.5.1.1. Pro:
        Private investment in the arts offers better support for the economic growth of communities [525] than other sources of funding.

        1.6.5.1.2. Con:
        Private investment is a less stable method of funding and creates a number of issues and concerns for arts organizations (pp. 13–14 [526]).

      1.6.5.2. Con:
      If this were true, then arts programs would only require short-term government support to drive investment, yet many arts programs receive recurring government support.

        1.6.5.2.1. Pro:
        Recurring government support can lead to arts programs becoming complacent and eschewing innovation rather than seeking new ways to become self-supporting.

        1.6.5.2.2. Con:
        Different forms of art have distinct needs and opportunities; private investment is not suitable for all arts programs.

      1.6.5.3. Con:
      If government arts funding is sufficient to create arts programs that have a decent public presence and are generally well respected, then investors will likely see fewer opportunities for investing in them.

        1.6.5.3.1. Con:
        Arts programs that achieve their goals with public funds have demonstrated their effectiveness and may, therefore, be more attractive to some investors.

        1.6.5.3.2. Pro:
        Venture capital funding [527] is generally focused on the early stages of projects with a high potential for growth.

      1.6.5.4. Pro:
      Over a ten-year period, Korean government funding of film and culture led to significant venture crowd-in [528], resulting in explosive growth in several creative sectors.

        1.6.5.4.1. Pro:
        Government arts funding can be directed to industries and programs that align well with state economic development strategies (p. 4 [529]).

        1.6.5.4.2. Con:
        Venture capital funding strategies require a wholly different perspective and greater stakeholder involvement [530] than government funding and private donations.

    1.6.6. Pro:
    Historically, government funding has not been used to support the arts.

      1.6.6.1. Con:
      Many societies have a long tradition [531] of extensively supporting the arts with public funds.

        1.6.6.1.1. Con:
        There is an equally long tradition of concern that the controversy over arts funding distracts the government [532] and its citizens from more pressing matters.

        1.6.6.1.2. Pro:
        US presidents have typically been publicly supportive of government funding for the arts.

          1.6.6.1.2.1. Pro:
          President Eisenhower, an avid painter [533], was a staunch supporter [534] of government funding for the arts.

          1.6.6.1.2.2. Pro:
          President Kennedy sought to preserve American heritage through the creation of a federal arts council [535].

          1.6.6.1.2.3. Con:
          President Trump's 2018 federal budget cuts all but eliminated [536] government funding for the arts.

          1.6.6.1.2.4. Con:
          President George W. Bush cut US government funding for the Arts in Education program for eight consecutive years [537].

      1.6.6.2. Con:
      This is an appeal to tradition [538]; an historical lack of government support for arts programs is an invalid reason for not funding them in the present or future.

        1.6.6.2.1. Pro:
        Increased government funding of arts programs could be a marked improvement over historical approaches.

        1.6.6.2.2. Con:
        Traditions often reflect social values (pp. 1178–1179 [539]); it is likely that government funding of arts programs would be common if society deemed them to be important.

      1.6.6.3. Pro:
      Many major orchestras existed prior to receiving government funding [540] and would continue to exist if government funding were to end.

        1.6.6.3.1. Pro:
        With the exception of specific government-requested performances [541], the renowned Vienna Philharmonic Orchestra has operated successfully without government funding [542] since 1842.

        1.6.6.3.2. Con:
        Most orchestras around the world operate on a deficit [543].

      1.6.6.4. Pro:
      In Western Europe, art has prospered for centuries due to the private patronage culture [544] of artists.

        1.6.6.4.1. Pro:
        As the role of patronage has declined [545], the arts have increasingly been supported by the broader public.

        1.6.6.4.2. Pro:
        The arts have been funded by wealthy private patrons since at least fifteenth-century Florence, where the Medici family [546] funded most artistic endeavors.

        1.6.6.4.3. Con:
        Patronage of the arts by the state and individual political figures has been common since the Middle Ages [547].

There’s even a word for this phenomenon: elderspeak. Examples of elderspeak include a doctor talking to the senior’s child rather than to the senior themselves, or a store clerk assuming an older customer needs them to speak very…loudly…and…slowly.

Not only is it demeaning, but this behaviour can have unintended consequences on a senior’s health and relationships with others."

[40] https://reasonstobecheerful.world/reducing-incarceration-no-prosecuting-minor-non-violent-crimes/
"One way of reducing mass incarceration is to simply start ignoring certain laws. Some 80 percent of cases filed nationally are for misdemeanors. These are the types of crimes that are often victimless, but that can mess up the life of the person prosecuted for them. A few places have addressed this in the most straightforward way possible: by not automatically prosecuting these crimes. What has happened as a result? Studies have shown that these places reduced their prison populations without putting the public at risk. Crime did not go up. In fact, in many cases, it went down. And, surprisingly, often not just for misdemeanors."

In his essay Art and Democracy, written in 1945 after years of promoting art to the public, Kenneth Clark came to the conclusion that art was ‘incurably aristocratic’, and (more controversially), ‘an outstanding example of the rule of the many by the few’. This is hardly wall text material for an art museum nowadays, but the point still stands: art is about an individual vision imposed on the world. No work of art worth looking at has ever been created by majority consensus. Artists are benign despots. Rather than a source of inspiration, democracy is, for artists, the worst possible situation to find themselves in."

[89] https://canadianart.ca/essays/tearing-down-the-gates/
"My teachers were all about abstract art. I felt like an imposter for not being into it. I was treated like an outsider because they did not like my figurative paintings. At that time, I was inspired by sci-fi and fantasy, and much more interested in H.R. Giger than Jean-Paul Riopelle. My art was deemed less worthy, especially since at that time I didn’t care about impressing anyone or acquiring cultural capital. This was my first experience of gatekeeping in the arts world.

Gatekeeping is the act of controlling or limiting access to an institution. Its consequences are the formation of an elite, and the safekeeping of knowledge and positions in their hands. I would argue that most of the time it is not overt, it is covert. It is in discourses, norms, expectations and behaviours that people co-opt to become, and to stay, part of this social group."

[92] https://www.getty.edu/art/exhibitions/renaissance_nude/inner.html
"The nude—the unclothed or partially clothed human body—has been featured in European art for millennia. After 1400, with the waning of the Middle Ages, artists depicted nudes as increasingly three-dimensional, vibrant, and lifelike— in short, more immediate and real. They employed diverse means: in Italy through a return to the models of ancient Greek and Roman art, and in northern Europe through refinements to the technique of painting in oils that enabled painters to capture textures—of flesh, of hair, of the sparkle in an eye—with unprecedented truth to nature. In concert with new scientific approaches, artists across Europe studied nature—including the human body—with increasing specificity and deliberation.

The meaningful depiction of the human form became the highest aspiration for artists, and their efforts often resulted in figures of notable sensuality. For Christians [...] most of European society at the time—the nude body could be disturbing, arousing personal desire."

This was especially important in the midst of a historical moment where rising tides of nationalism in other parts of the world were leading governments (run by figures like Stalin and Hitler) to direct and control artworks that would project their own ideological frameworks to their citizens and the world."

[131] https://theconversation.com/why-public-funding-of-the-arts-should-always-be-temporary-59469
"Government funding for arts organisations should always be viewed as temporary because there are significant costs and dangers that arise from an implicit contract to permanent funding.
[...]
Another reason is that turnover of funding creates competition that incentivises all parties to bring their best work forward. Those in favour of permanent funding say it brings certainty to enable long term planning. But in reality it has the opposite effect by removing all consequences for institutions that fail to engage in effective long term strategic planning for growth.

Permanent funding removes the hard incentive to develop new business models to grow the operation or organisation beyond the initial period. It militates against long term thinking.

So how long is temporary? This is the question we should be asking. One or two years is too short to get anything started, and to get beyond initial experimentation. Eight to ten years is probably too long, as it invites complacency."
[132] https://www.epi.org/publication/creating-jobs-and-economic-security/

This aggregate demand management is the task of macroeconomic policy—specifically, fiscal, monetary, and exchange rate policies. If aggregate demand is too low, no other job creation strategy can work at scale until the shortfall is fixed."
[133] https://www.thebalancemoney.com/do-tax-cuts-create-jobs-3306325
"Payroll tax cuts are the most cost-effective ways to increase jobs because they lower the cost of labor. These cuts create jobs in four specific ways:

1. Companies with popular products immediately use the savings to hire more workers.

2. Other companies use the savings to reduce prices. That increases demand, which necessitates hiring more workers. 

3. Some firms use tax savings to allow them to buy more goods. This benefits manufacturers.

4. Many businesses use the cuts to raise wages to retain good workers. The workers spend more, increasing demand.

According to the CBO, every $1 million in payroll tax cuts creates 13 new jobs.

The payroll tax cuts specifically targeted for new hires is the most cost-effective tax cut. Every $1 million in targeted payroll tax cuts creates 18 new jobs. It lowers the cost of new employees when compared to existing workers or investment in new equipment. That changes employers’ decision-making in favor of new hires."
[134] https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/j.1467-9787.1993.tb00222.x

The results indicate that metropolitan areas which are more diverse experience lower unemployment rates and less instability than areas which are less diverse."
[135] https://www.arts.gov/sites/default/files/ADP6-6_Impact_New_Demand_Arts.pdf
"If demand for arts and culture increases, then production increases as well. In so doing, however, producers of arts and culture purchase additional goods and services from their suppliers. Consequently, an initial increase in demand for arts and culture results in diminishing rounds of new spending—a multiplier effect.
 
The 2012 ACPSA multipliers indicate that every $1 increase in the demand for arts and culture generates $1.69 in total output; for every job created from new demand for the arts, an additional 1.62 positions are also created."

[183] https://www.thebalancemoney.com/government-subsidies-definition-farm-oil-export-etc-3305788
"Subsidies are cash grants or loans given to particular industries to promote growth or impact business and consumer behavior.
[...]
Most subsidies are cash grants or loans that the government gives to businesses. It encourages activities the government wishes to promote. The subsidy depends on the amount of the goods or services provided.

For example, the U.S. government has long supported the agricultural industry through cash subsidies given to farmers to help control the supply of essential crops.
[...]
The government can subsidize an industry in a number of ways—through direct funding, loans, tax breaks or credits, the elimination of fees or penalties, etc.—but all of these amount to the same thing: financial support. And simply put, when an industry has more money (or fewer expenses) it can do more business."

[...] This may be easily translated into a political context. Atwood’s The handmaid’s tale may be thought of as a carefully constructed thought experiment of a life lived under extreme misogyny; Public Enemy’s Fear of a Black Planet may be said to conceptually clarify the concept ‘systemic racism’ by giving clear examples of it. "Constraints on Governments Powers (Factor 1 of the WJP Rule of Law Index) measures the extent to which those who govern are bound by law. It comprises the means, both constitutional and institutional, by which the powers of the government and its officials and agents are limited and held accountable under the law. It also includes non-governmental checks on the government’s power, such as a free and independent press. Governmental checks take many forms; they do not operate solely in systems marked by a formal separation of powers, nor are they necessarily codified in law. What is essential, however, is that authority is distributed, whether by formal rules or by convention, in a manner that ensures that no single organ of government has the practical ability to exercise unchecked power."

The city decided to revamp the lift station as part of its WATERSHED+ initiative. The new lift station, completed in 2015, features a map of LED bar lights that change colour based on how fast water inside is pumping.

Upon opening, the new station proved controversial, with area residents creating the “poop palace” moniker.

"I estimate that private charitable contributions to arts organizations increased by 60 cents to a dollar due to a major funding cut to the National Endowment for the Arts (NEA) during the mid-1990s. These increases, however, also coincided with, on average, a 25 cent increase in fund-raising expenditures by arts organizations for every dollar decrease in government grants. The estimate of crowding out found in this paper is large, particularly for a study using a micro-data set. I argue that an appropriate interpretation of an estimate of a crowding out parameter, in general, depends crucially on the context."
[291] https://www.federalreserve.gov/pubs/feds/2008/200810/200810pap.pdf

To preview the results, I find that private charitable contributions to arts organizations increased by 60 cents to a dollar for every dollar decrease in government grants. There was also a concomitant 25 cent increase in fund-raising expenses when the NEA experienced its budget cuts.
[...]
If organizations fund-raised to the point where a dollar of fund-raising expenditures brought in a dollar of private contributions, we can surmise that the increase in fund-raising was responsible for roughly 25 cents of the increase in private giving, leaving crowding out net-of-fund-raising at about 80 cents."

Referring back to table 2, the indirect effect of government support on private support is a significant issue in regards to state support for smaller orchestras. This is the one area that government support has a significant and negative impact on fundraising. This would make sense in that the direct impact of state support positively impacts private giving, lessening the need for fundraising. Therefore, the best nonprofit mission statements are a succinct encapsulation of:

-Why does your nonprofit exist
-Whom it serves
-How it serves them

Some nonprofits struggle to differentiate between a mission and a vision statement.

Although both mission and vision statements capture the essence of your nonprofit’s beliefs and values, a vision statement explains the overall goal of your organization looking into the future, while the mission statement outlines the present plan to realize the vision."

"If the donor intends on taking a tax deduction of more than $5,000, they will need to get an independent qualified appraisal within 60 day of the contribution date to do so. The Treasury Regulations are specific about what makes an appraiser 'qualified', but generally the appraiser should be someone who has credentials in the relevant field in order to make appraisals on the type of property being valued. Once the appraisal has been conducted, the donor must complete IRS Form 8283 since the value of the gift exceeds $5,000. The appraisal should be attached to the form and both the donee organization and the appraiser should sign. Additional requirements apply to artwork donated with claimed value of $50,000 or more. If the organization disposes of the gift within three years of the date it was received, it must file Form 8282 within 125 days after disposition."

The emphasis, as you can read in its first report of 1964-65, was that the arts ought to be recognized “as a vital part of our national life, and not a luxury.” The initial commission that studied it had come to the conclusion that the relentless focus on practical subjects in education had led to widespread discontent with the materialism of US society. This, at least, seems to be implicit in the core meaning of expression: the outer manifestation of an inner state. Art as a representation of outer existence (admittedly “seen through a temperament”) has been replaced by art as an expression of humans’ inner life."

The New Oxford American Dictionary defines “politics” as “the activities associated with the governance of a country or other area, especially the debate or conflict among individuals or parties having or hoping to achieve power.” I think that’s a reasonable definition, and it puts some clear boundaries on what is necessarily political. Politics may influence many areas of life, but I think to say that every work of art is bound up in politics really stretches the definition of politics to the point where it becomes meaningless."

As Thomas Jefferson said, “The care of life and happiness is the sole legitimate objective of government”.

Corresponding to this was the basic proposition of moral philosophy – that the right way to live is to create the most happiness and the least misery that you can in the world around you. That was the lodestar for moral action."

Specific mediums and forms of artistic expression have changed throughout human history, but for the most part, art falls into one of the following seven classical forms. Each different form of art is experienced differently and affects our emotions and feelings. 

-Painting
-Sculpture
-Literature
-Architecture
-Cinema
-Music
-Theater"

Break this rule in the wrong opera house (La Scala in Milan is notorious for this) and the public backlash can be scathing."

This article delineates the presence of Albanian cinema in the People’s Republic of China (PRC) from 1949 to 1976, when geopolitical realignments reconfigured the socialist camp, with special attention to Albanian cinema’s position within the filmscape in Mao’s China. Drawing on official news reports, movie reviews and recently published personal reminiscences, and employing the lens of the captive audience, it teases out the multifaceted reception of Albanian films, including the types of engagement that deviate from and challenge the then-prevailing revolutionary discourses. The study thus helps to demystify a reductive understanding of the socio-political significance of Albanian cinema in China."

[423] https://undsci.berkeley.edu/who-pays-for-science/
"In a perfect world, money wouldn’t matter — all scientific studies (regardless of funding source) would be completely objective. But of course, in the real world, funding may introduce biases — for example, when the backer has a stake in the study’s outcome. A pharmaceutical company paying for a study of a new depression medication, for example, might influence the study’s design or interpretation in ways that subtly favor the drug that they’d like to market. There is evidence that some biases like this do occur. Drug research sponsored by the pharmaceutical industry is more likely to end up favoring the drug under consideration than studies sponsored by government grants or charitable organizations. Similarly, nutrition research sponsored by the food industry is more likely to end up favoring the food under consideration than independently funded research."

"A lengthy battle over public funding for the arts ended yesterday when the Supreme Court ruled that the federal government can consider general standards of decency and the "values of the American public" in deciding which projects should receive cash grants.

The ruling came in a dispute that began nearly a decade ago, when funding by the National Endowment for the Arts for works with controversial sexual and religious themes led to the 1990 enactment of a law imposing a decency standard. The law became a rallying point for artists in many disciplines who claimed their ideas were being chilled and their rights to free expression violated.

But in its 8 to 1 ruling, the court upheld that statute yesterday, saying it saw no evidence that the decency standard suppressed expression, even of work that might be thought unpopular or offensive."
[435] https://www.aclu.org/other/freedom-expression-arts-and-entertainment
"The Supreme Court has interpreted the First Amendment's protection of artistic expression very broadly.
[...]
Two fundamental principles come into play whenever a court must decide a case involving freedom of expression. The first is "content neutrality"-- the government cannot limit expression just because any listener, or even the majority of a community, is offended by its content. In the context of art and entertainment, this means tolerating some works that we might find offensive, insulting, outrageous -- or just plain bad. 

The second principle is that expression may be restricted only if it will clearly cause direct and imminent harm to an important societal interest. The classic example is falsely shouting fire in a crowded theater and causing a stampede."
[436] https://ncac.org/news/blog/how-obscene-is-this-the-decency-clause-turns-20
"When it was founded in the 1960s, the National Endowment for the Arts (NEA), a central part of its mission was to support individuals and institutions producing edgy and innovative artwork.

Twenty years ago, as a result of pressures on behalf of Republicans in Congress and the religious right, Congress amended the statute governing the NEA to require that it consider “general standards of respect and decency for the diverse beliefs and values of the American public” when awarding art grants. A few years later the NEA budget was slashed and individual artists’ grants were eliminated. In 1998 the “decency” amendment was ruled constitutional by the US Supreme Court because it added just one more consideration to grant giving and did not oppose specific viewpoint-based restrictions."

"Anyone who is aware of the progression of the art world as a social and economic force over the past three or four decades, either through lived experience or significant research, understands that art is now subjected to the same economic totalism as any other enterprise. Since the late seventies, art has become increasingly identified with its commodity status. Art and consequently artists have become increasingly acquiescent to publicity and media exposure. Just as the stock market depends to a large extent on media manipulation in order to keep investments afloat, so the art market depends on principle vehicles of exposure in order to keep the prices on a steady incline. In spite of what critics have to say, it is the exposure of the art that counts."

"One idea is for consumers to support the artists they love through a partial ownership model, where a percentage of the profits from the sale of an artwork is returned to the originating artist. A good example of this approach is Dada.nyc, a platform that supports collaborative art created digitally on their web site. Dada.nyc uses blockchain technology to enforce intellectual property protection for artists and to securely track transactions so that every initial sale or subsequent resale of artwork results in an automatic partial payment to the artist.

Another suggested approach is to support artists through a crowdfunding model, where consumers can choose to fund creative projects by the artists they wish to support. Bailey is a vocal advocate of this approach, stating in his 2019 art market predictions piece that: “we will soon see a combination of the proven distribution and consumption model of Instagram paired with patronage models like Patreon and Kickstarter.”"

"For decades, the bulk of Indian music production, discovery and consumption has been driven by the film industry. Music is specifically written for movies, and the actors—rather than musicians—become the face of that music, making for a largely song-driven market.

But it has expanded into more of an artist-driven market over the past decade-plus, in large part thanks to YouTube. Consumers, primed for video-based consumption of music, began discovering young, independent artists across pop, rock and hip-hop there, said Times Music Chief Operating Officer Mandar Thakur.

That shift was helped by the penetration of cheap smartphones, which led to growth in India for audiostreaming services, such as JioSaavn, Amazon Music and Apple Music. Spotify, which launched in India early in 2019, in particular has helped market individual artists, not only from India but also from Indian populations around the world, including Punjabi acts out of Canada, said Mr. Thakur."

Many people are just blown away that people are interested in their traditions and their history. One artist said when she got back to her village, everybody wanted to touch her visa.

[452] https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/aesthetic-concept/
"Introduced into the philosophical lexicon during the Eighteenth Century, the term ‘aesthetic’ has come to designate, among other things, a kind of object, a kind of judgment, a kind of attitude, a kind of experience, and a kind of value. For the most part, aesthetic theories have divided over questions particular to one or another of these designations: whether artworks are necessarily aesthetic objects; how to square the allegedly perceptual basis of aesthetic judgments with the fact that we give reasons in support of them; how best to capture the elusive contrast between an aesthetic attitude and a practical one; whether to define aesthetic experience according to its phenomenological or representational content; how best to understand the relation between aesthetic value and aesthetic experience."

[462] https://www.childrensmusicworkshop.com/advocacy/nea/
"Yet Endowment funding is just a drop in the bucket compared to giving to the arts by private citizens. For example, in 1996, the Metropolitan Opera of New York received $390,000 from the Endowment, a federal subsidy that totals only 0.29 percent of the Opera’s annual income of $133 million-and amounts to less than the ticket revenue for a single sold-out performance. The growth of private-sector charitable giving in recent years has rendered NEA funding relatively insignificant to the arts community. Overall giving to the arts last year totaled almost $10 billion-up from $6.5 billion in 1991-dwarfing the NEA’s federal subsidy. This 40 percent increase in private giving occurred during the same period that the NEA budget was reduced by 40 percent from approximately $170 million to $99.5 million. Thus, as conservatives had predicted, cutting the federal NEA subsidy coincided with increased private support for the arts and culture."

When we hold in our minds a certain ideal of what a creative person should look or act like, we put unnecessary obstacles in our path and do a disservice to the magic of creativity.

"Against the backdrop of the Great Depression, President Franklin Delano Roosevelt's administration created the Public Works of Art Project—the first federal government program to support the arts nationally. Federal officials in the 1930s understood how essential art was to sustaining America's spirit. Artists from across the United States who participated in the program, which lasted only six months from mid-December 1933 to June 1934, were encouraged to depict "the American Scene." The Public Works of Art Project not only paid artists to embellish public buildings, but also provided them with a sense of pride in serving their country."

He worked as a painter, printmaker, and ceramicist, but is best known as a sculptor. Under the auspices of the Federal Arts Project (FAP), Johnson carved a 22-foot-long redwood relief of musicians, animals, birds, and plants as a screen for a pipe organ in the music hall of the California School for the Blind in Berkeley.

The organ screen was removed from its original building after the California School of the Blind moved to a new campus in 1980 and was not seen by the public for more than 30 years. The exhibition presents this monumental sculpture, acquired by the Huntington in 2011, for the first time since its restoration, along with details of the restoration project."

[486] https://www.theartstory.org/definition/federal-art-project-of-the-works-progress-administration/
"During its years of operation, the government-funded Federal Art Project (FAP) of the Works Progress Administration (WPA) hired hundreds of artists who collectively created more than 100,000 paintings and murals and over 18,000 sculptures to be found in municipal buildings, schools, and hospitals in all of the 48 states. Additionally, nearly 100 community art centers throughout the country provided art classes for children and developing artists. The FAP was part of President Franklin Roosevelt's New Deal during the Great Depression, in which he sought to put as many unemployed Americans back to work as possible and to buoy morale of the citizens. Some of the 20th century's greatest visual artists were employed by the FAP, along with many nascent Abstract Expressionists."
[487] https://www.smithsonianmag.com/arts-culture/1934-the-art-of-the-new-deal-132242698/

The great majority of them were younger than 40 when they enrolled, by which time most artists have either made their reputation or switched to another line of work. Some, it appears, would be almost completely unknown today if the Smithsonian, in the 1960s, hadn't received the surviving PWAP artworks from government agencies that had displayed them. "They did their best work for the nation," Broun says, and then they disappeared below the national horizon to the realm of regional or local artist.

"The art they produced was rather conservative, and it wouldn't be looked at by most critics today," says Francis O'Connor, a New York City-based scholar and author of the 1969 book Federal Suppport (sp) for the Visual Arts."

[507] https://www.theartblog.org/2020/10/how-do-we-value-art/
"Art is irrefutably valuable when used as an indicator of wealth, a status symbol, or when it can be resold at a profit. However, we do not value artists for their time, skills, knowledge, or expertise. Time: if paid by the hour, a graphic designer who is efficient is punished. Skills: artists are expected to maintain a day job and pursue their art career in the evenings and weekends. Expertise: you will not see an art expert on the news; people think they know enough about art already (abstract art? my kid could paint that).

If art is an area of time, skill, and expertise, it ticks off three value boxes. So why does art not come to mind when we ask most people what they value? One huge reason art is not valued is because it is not accessible. It is treated not as a part of life, but as a non-essential feature of life, reserved for the few, but not for everyone. Art can and should be for everyone. By not valuing artists, we devalue art. Art encompasses both maker and object."
[508] https://academic.oup.com/book/32621/chapter-abstract/270485142?redirectedFrom=fulltext
"Philosopher Denis Dutton believed that we judge an artwork in part by the kind of achievement it represents. Something created without effort constitutes a different achievement from something created with much effort. One might assume that a work completed without much effort would be most valued because such a work is evidence of a greater talent than one completed with greater effort. However, research shows the opposite. All else being equal (and without priming people to think about talent), more effort leads to a more positive judgment. The history behind the work is part and parcel of the work. We cannot help but allow process to affect our evaluation of the product."

The empirical evidence for a large sample of countries at different stages of development since 1960 to the present shows that an increase of 10 percentage points in the ratio of private investment to GDP corresponds to an increase of 3.1 points in the long-term growth rate of per capita income, higher than the elasticity of 2.7 obtained between total investment and growth."

"The appeal to tradition fallacy occurs when someone claims that a particular action or belief must be good or true because it is traditional.

In other words, it is based on the false assumption that if something has been done a certain way for a long time (that is, traditionally), it is necessarily the right way of doing it.

As John Locke, a well-known English philosopher, pointed out:

“New opinions are always suspected, and usually opposed, without any other reason but because they are not already common.”"

"They all run an operating deficit, in the sense that the money they earn from concerts, records and so forth does not cover their expenses," he told The Money.

There are many reasons for this, but chief among them is that the expense of running an orchestra is enormous and virtually impossible to bring down."
[544] https://newrepublic.com/article/142925/americans-hostile-state-funded-art

[545] https://www.tubefilter.com/2016/06/16/history-future-funding-art/
"Smetime between the Renaissance and In Rainbows, funding art moved away from the old patronage model to a more publicly-supported system. Anyone–not just the extremely wealthy–could find and support art in museums, theatres, galleries, and the like. Artists could create elaborate works of their own choosing and willing buyers would fork over big bucks to have a piece of it.

As art became increasingly institutionalized, it begat several new ways to earn money and a hungry subset of middlemen wedged themselves between the artist and the patron. Publishing companies, agents, publicists, producers, movie studios, record labels and a number of other professions cropped up as a way to maximize and share in the wealth of creators by managing some of the more business-focused parts of the artist’s work."
[546] https://www.nga.gov/features/slideshows/patrons-and-artists-in-late-15th-century-florence.html
"Among the greatest patrons in 15th-century Florence were members of the powerful Medici family, who ruled as princes, though the city was, in name, a republic. The works in this tour date from the time of Lorenzo de' Medici, the Magnificent, whom Machiavelli called "the greatest patron of literature and art that any prince has ever been." Although Lorenzo himself commissioned relatively few major works, he was an important arbiter of taste. An avid collector of Greek and Roman antiquities, he helped imprint the Florentine Renaissance with the humanism of the ancient world.

One of the artists employed by the Medici was Botticelli, a member of Lorenzo's circle of poets and scholars. Botticelli's lyrical paintings matched the cerebral refinement of Florence's humanists, especially the Neoplatonic philosophers, who saw beauty as a way to approach an understanding of the divine."
[547] https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1002/9781118396957.wbemlb108
"The system of patronage was one of the most significant social and cultural structures in the Middle Ages. The relationships forged between patrons and their clients were based not only on financial support, but also political and personal influence. Men and women from a wide variety of social backgrounds participated in the patronage system; their important contributions to this system included endowing medieval colleges, commissioning individual translations or artworks, and supporting writers and artists for many years."
